Executive summary
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                             DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



A record 1.57 million international passengers arrived at Cape Town International in
FY25/26, against 1.16 million in FY19/20, before the pandemic.1 Its average load factor of
79% in 2025 was the highest of South Africa's five largest airports.2 Between 2015 and 2025
the airport added 17 mainline international routes, more than any of eight peer airports.3 The
recovery is complete. The next stage is harder, because at the peak the system that
delivered it is now full.

Interest is not what holds the province back. About 97 million high-value travellers in 34 markets are
interested in South Africa, yet only about 1.4 million convert into arrivals.4 Seats and bookings stand in the
way. Eleven of 27 international routes run above 80% load across the year, and at the summer peak one
runway caps movements at about 30 an hour.5 In winter, carriers pull seats: June holds 6.4% of the year's
international capacity, against 10.8% in December.6 Beyond the Metro, most rural properties are missing
from the online travel agents where visitors book.7

On the growth model's count, arrivals can grow from about 1.5 million in 2025 to 2.5 million by 2035, if the
province wins the seats.8 That makes this strategy a supply programme before it is a marketing
programme.


The province earns a premium from a narrow base

The Western Cape earns more per visitor than the rest of South Africa. The province takes about a
quarter of national foreign spend from about a seventh of the arrivals. Its foreign visitors spend roughly 1.6
times the national average.9 That is about US$890 per arrival, against US$500 for the rest of South
Africa.10 Foreign direct spend rose 14% in a year, from R22.7bn in FY2024 to R25.9bn in FY2025. 11

Yet the premium rests on a few markets, six months and one metro. Five markets supply 63% of overseas
arrivals, and the UK, Germany and the USA alone 52%.12 Most arrive through someone else's hub: the
routes through Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul carry 53% of arrivals on the ten largest routes. 13
October to March carries between 61% and 62% of overseas visitor travel.14 The Cape Metro holds 20,281
of the province's 50,332 tourism assets, or 40.3%, and visitors disperse from there. 15 Growth now needs
new markets, new seasons and new regions.


The limits are physical and institutional

Five constraints cap the case, and none of them is a lack of interest in the province.

The gateway. The growth model draws air access as a line at 1.8 million inbound seats, and the base
path crosses it around 2030.16 The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap. In practice the

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                             DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



runway binds first, at peak hours. Runway relief comes from about 2033.17 Airports Company South Africa
(ACSA) now schedules the realigned runway for December 2029, subject to contract award.18 This
strategy plans on about 2033 and counts the earlier date as upside. George Airport is domestic only, and
Cape Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named carrier.19

Full routes. Five of the eleven full routes are mature long-haul services, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta
at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at 83.0%.20 Carriers on full routes raise fares before they
add seats.21

Seasons. The green season, April to September, is a seat problem and a demand problem at once: seats
and visitors fall away together. Connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season". 22 That ties winter
seats to the hub carriers' own priorities.

Reach. Accessibility falls from 4.7 out of 5 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and one touring
route of six is rated Ready. Beyond the Metro and the Garden Route the province is effectively car-only. 23

Levers held elsewhere. National government and its state-owned entities hold visas, air service
agreements and the airport and port programmes.24 Mobility mandates are split across three tiers, and no
approved method exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs.25

Most of the five sit outside a marketing budget, and the runway and air rights sit outside the province.


What 2.5 million arrivals would take

Wesgro's original ask was larger: a strategy to "double international tourist arrivals to the Western Cape
by 2035".26 The evidence reframes that ambition as growth to 2.5 million. The gateway sets the pace in
the summer peak, and demand and conversion set it in the green season and beyond the Metro. The
growth model draws three paths from 1,498,829 arrivals in 2025.27 By 2035 the conservative path reaches
1,822,634, the base path 2,175,778 (about 2.2 million) and the accelerated path 2,516,282.

The paths differ by intervention, not by demand potential. The base path needs Emirates, Qatar, Ethiopian
and Turkish Airlines to keep adding capacity and Wesgro's programmes to expand. The accelerated path
also needs direct India and China routes and dedicated activation in India, China, the USA and the Gulf.
Without confirmed routes, it reverts to base.28

Figure ES.1. Arrivals paths to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21. The 2031 base value and the
accelerated and conservative milestones are computed (section 5.3).

On the eleven full routes, marketing cannot make up for missing seats, but on the hub routes, at 65% to
72% load, it can.29 The hub markets supply 206,584 of the 676,949 arrivals the base path adds, the
largest share.30

The base path implies growth of 3.8% a year and the accelerated path 5.3%, against a global norm of 3%
to 5%.31 Yet in 2024, while recovery was still running, Chile grew 40.4% and Singapore 21%, against 11%
for the world.32 The fastest growers aimed institutions, marketing and air access at one binding
constraint.33 For Chile that constraint was long-haul access. The Western Cape's has the same shape:
seats into one gateway airport.


The strategy makes three shifts

In Pillar 1, Reshape demand, the work moves from awareness to conversion. The province is under-sold
in its growth markets and hard to book outside its core. Three of its six focus areas aim at conversion:
green-season seats, bookable product and hub traffic already in the air. About 101,000 Indian and
Chinese travellers a year already reach Cape Town, mainly through Gulf and African hubs.34 Singapore
shows how a hub can turn transit into visits, and Melbourne uses its events calendar to steady demand. 35

In Pillar 2, Transform supply, the work moves from new attractions to seats and finished experiences. The
pillar keeps one capacity entry: frequency and larger aircraft on the full routes ahead of runway relief. In
2027 and 2028, Initiative I6 designs a public-private air access fund to back it (section 4.3), the lesson
drawn from Chile.36 Five more cover reach, product and experience. At the flagship sites, Boulders is near

Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                             13
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                      DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



carrying capacity and Cableway queues run up to three hours.37 The task is to turn existing assets into
complete visitor experiences.38

In Pillar 3, Build enabling capability, the work moves from separate mandates to shared decisions, capital
and data. The pillar answers four questions. First, who benefits: twenty township and rural communities
hold 18.4% of the province's residents but receive a traced 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend. 39
Second, who pays: the capital pipeline funds airports, ports, roads and rail, but none of it reaches tourism
product.40 Third, who decides: with visas and air service agreements held nationally, the province leads by
escalation.41 Fourth, who measures: more than twenty bodies produce tourism data, yet no one holds a
single view of visitor behaviour.42 New Zealand recommends yield, not arrivals, as the regional measure.43

The three pillars make one case. Demand work fills seats only if the seats exist, and supply work pays
only if the product can be booked. Both need the enabling base to last.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                 DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




Seats and bookings move first

Air access and bookability move first, because every other lever runs through them. The plan runs in three
phases. Activate, from 2027 to 2028, holds and adds seats on the full routes, counts the bookability gap
and sets up the institutions the rest depends on. Gate 1 closes the phase at the end of 2028, two years
before the base path crosses the seat line.44 Expand, from 2029 to 2031, scales what works and meets
the seat line at the 2030 mid-term review. Sustain, from 2032 to 2035, takes direct route cases to carriers
as runway capacity arrives.

Eighteen initiatives carry the plan, one per focus area except where one owner delivers two. Wesgro is
accountable for twelve. The Department of Economic Development and Tourism (DEDAT), the
Department of Mobility, municipalities, local tourism organisations (LTOs) and site operators lead the other
six. Thirteen draw mainly on Wesgro's operating budget. None has yet been costed, and setting the rand
bands is the first funding task (section 4.6).

Twelve actions in three strands can start in the first 24 months, nine of them inside Wesgro's current
mandate and budget.

Seats and season come first, from the first half of 2027. Cape Town Air Access holds winter seats through
joint marketing with carriers on the full routes and tracks the runway quarterly (I6, I2). The Convention
Bureau publishes a green-season events calendar and aims bids at May to September (I2).

Governance and baselines also start then. The Wesgro CEO's office and DEDAT constitute the Tourism
Growth Steerco and table the first national escalation agenda (I15, I17). Wesgro's Research unit agrees a
data-sharing protocol with ACSA, SA Tourism, Stats SA and Cape Town Tourism (I16). It also runs a
bookability census by district and requests visitor counts from the five flagship sites (I3, I11).

Product and inclusion follow in the second half of 2027. Wesgro's Tourism unit packages the Garden
Route and Route 62 with a shuttle transfer (I8, I9). It also packages the Southern Line as a visitor product
with the City of Cape Town and the Passenger Rail Agency of South Africa (I8). It chooses two or three
pilot communities, and from the first half of 2028 the LTOs give each community one live booking route
before any promotion (I12, I3). Each action opens a seat, a baseline or a booking path a later initiative
needs. Owners and start dates are proposals for Steerco to confirm (section 4.5).


How the Board will track progress

The Board will judge the plan on seven impact indicators, and today only arrivals and spend carry a
modelled value for 2035. Beneath them sit twenty-five outcome and thirty-six output indicators. Steerco
receives a quarterly scorecard and the Board an annual outlook against both paths. Gates at the end of
2028 and 2031, a mid-term review in 2030 and a terminal evaluation in 2035 decide what each phase
funds.

Jobs wait on the labour analysis, so the ambition of 300,000 tourism-linked jobs is tested before it
becomes a target. The green-season share starts at 38.1%, but the share outside the Metro has no
baseline until the data protocol delivers one in 2027 (section 5.3). Gate 1 turns the missing baselines into
targets, so the first two years must produce data as well as results.


What could knock it off course

Ten of the fourteen material risks sit outside Wesgro's control, so the response to most is early warning
and a route to the tier that can act. One is rated high on both likelihood and impact: restrictive bilateral air
agreements and the US carrier cap. It calls for an escalation agenda with stated asks, plus route work
inside existing rights.

Six are rated medium likelihood and high impact, in three pairs; first, two sit with the national tier and
ACSA. Runway relief may slip past about 2033, so its milestones are tracked quarterly. Visa processing
may not improve, and the escalation agenda carries it. Second, two sit in the markets. A Gulf hub
disruption would cut connecting capacity, so hub work is spread across all four hubs. A downturn in the
UK, Germany and the USA would hit 52% of overseas arrivals, so activation shifts towards Africa and the
hub markets. Third, two sit closer to delivery. Safety incidents could deter visitors, so safety gates come
before any pilot launch. A cut to Wesgro's operating budget would hit thirteen initiatives, so rand bands
and a stop list are due before Gate 1.

Two more are rated high likelihood and medium impact. Climate events that close sites and routes call for
a climate risk register. The loss of LTO funding, which MFMA Circular No. 131 has disrupted, calls for
stable funding agreements with a development mandate. Likelihood and impact are Letsema's
assessment, and Steerco reviews the register each quarter (section 4.8).


What the Board is asked to decide

Five decisions start the plan, and two recommendations pass partners the levers Wesgro does not hold.

The Wesgro Board decides:

   1. Adopt growth to 2.5 million arrivals by 2035 as the plan's goal, with the base and accelerated paths
      as its targets.
   2. Constitute the Tourism Growth Steerco, co-chaired by Wesgro's CEO and DEDAT's Head of
      Department with Cape Town Tourism as a standing member, and confirm the twelve initiative leads
      that sit with Wesgro.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                     19
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                          DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



   3. Approve the nine first-24-month actions that sit inside Wesgro's mandate and budget. Ask Wesgro
      Finance and DEDAT to set rand bands for every initiative, and to name what Wesgro deprioritises
      to fund them, before Gate 1. [Open item: Wesgro Finance to give an indicative rand band for the
      first 24 months.]
   4. Commission the bookability census in 2027, so the plan's missing baselines exist by Gate 1.
   5. Table the first national escalation agenda on visas, air service agreements and immigration
      processing at Cape Town International.
The Board recommends to the Provincial Minister, DEDAT and the municipalities:

   6. That DEDAT commission the tourism labour analysis in 2027 and lead I10, I13 and I18, and that the
      Department of Mobility lead I8.
   7. That the municipalities and LTOs lead and fund I12, and that the site operators lead I11.
Gate 1 is where the Board next decides. It will ask whether seats on the full routes have held, whether any
pilot has met its thresholds and whether the bookability baseline exists and is moving.

provincial employment baseline. Each is marked in square brackets where it occurs and listed with an owner in
the accompanying open-items register.

Chapter 1


Context
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                           DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




1.1 Mandate and objective
Wesgro commissioned a strategy to "double international tourist arrivals to the Western
Cape by 2035".45 The evidence reframes that goal as growth to 2.5 million arrivals, paced by
gateway capacity in the summer peak and by demand in the green season.

Wesgro's mandate and strategy
Wesgro promotes tourism, trade and investment for Cape Town and the Western Cape, so the tourism
goal sits inside a wider economic brief. Its mission is to work "together with partners, drive inclusive and
sustainable economic growth, strengthen competitiveness, and create jobs in Cape Town and the
Western Cape". Its vision is to "be a leading regional tourism, trade and investment promotion agency". 46
Two points in the mission shape this strategy: growth is a means to jobs, and Wesgro acts through
partners rather than holding the levers itself.

Four of Wesgro's eight strategy statements bear directly on this study.47 The first sets the destination
proposition: Cape Town and the Western Cape "top of mind as a leading business and tourism destination
with premium products and services". The fourth names new markets and buyers in China, ASEAN, India,
Brazil and the Middle East, "while maintaining a continued focus on established markets". The sixth
commits Wesgro to "Track and address systemic challenges influencing economic competitiveness", and
the seventh to "Provide economic data, insights & analytics for the Western Cape".

The study objective and its four ambitions
The objective, set in November 2025, is "To develop an evidence-based tourism strategy that will double
international tourist arrivals to the Western Cape by 2035". It continues: "through a holistic approach that
drives demand, strengthens supply and enables inclusive, sustainable and geographically dispersed
growth aligned with WESGRO's strategic priorities".48 The objective fixes the three pillars the report keeps
throughout: demand, supply and enablement. Of its terms, only the doubling did not survive the evidence.

Four headline ambitions sat under the objective: 2.8 million international visitors, 300,000 tourism-linked
jobs, 10,000 SMMEs onboarded and twice the airlift and cruise volumes.49 The strategy carries all four
forward. The evidence reframed the first and changed how the other three are measured.

Arrivals. The growth model tested the doubling and supports 2.2 million to 2.5 million by 2035. From
1,498,829 arrivals in 2025, its base case reaches 2,175,778 by 2035 and its accelerated case 2,516,282,
above a conservative floor of 1,822,634.50 The base case holds Wesgro's effort at 2025 intensity, with
selective frequency gains and continued hub expansion. The accelerated case needs confirmed direct
routes to India and China, above-baseline activation and more hub capacity into Cape Town; without the
routes, volume reverts to base.51 Growth to 2.5 million is that case rounded, and it is the goal the report
carries. The reframing was tabled with Wesgro on 14 October 2026 [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026,
page to be confirmed against the final deck].

Even the base case runs into the model's seat line. The model draws its seat line at 1.8 million inbound
seats, and the base case first passes it at the 2030 year point, at 1,802,929 arrivals. 52 Growth to 2.5

million is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing programme, and every chapter returns to
it.

Jobs. The ambition was 300,000 tourism-linked jobs, yet the evidence holds no Western Cape tourism
employment figure (section 2.4). The strategy takes jobs into Chapter 5 without a baseline. Wesgro and
DEDAT should commission a labour analysis in 2027 to set one (focus area 20, flagged as untested).

SMMEs. The ambition was 10,000 SMMEs onboarded, with no baseline or definition printed [Wesgro to
confirm the baseline count and definition of SMMEs onboarded]. The strategy treats it as community
inclusion, measured in visitor spend reaching priority communities rather than a count of firms (section 2.4
and Chapter 5).

Airlift and cruise. The ambition was twice the airlift and cruise volumes. Section 3.2.1 sets airlift as the
seats and routes needed to lift the model's 1.8 million seat line.53 It sets cruise as calls and ports [Cruise
Cape Town to confirm the call target and its definition]. The cruise baseline is 79 vessels in the 2024/25
season.54 Of the four ambitions, only arrivals has a target the evidence supports today. The other three go
forward with their baselines still to be fixed.

Wesgro's outcome metrics
Wesgro measures itself against six strategic outcomes, each with one metric.55 Two are tourism metrics:
800,000 to 1 million new international tourist arrivals and 161 business event bids secured, with an
estimated economic value of R4.5 billion. Three more cover a 120 million audience reached, R30 billion of
new investment and R9.1 billion of new export declarations. The sixth asks for a future-fit and trusted
Wesgro, with a clean audit, satisfied clients and low culture entropy. None of the six states a period
[Wesgro to confirm the period over which the six outcome metrics are measured].

The period decides how the arrivals metric compares with the growth case. Over ten years, 800,000 to 1
million new arrivals sits between the two paths, which add 676,949 and 1,017,453 from 2025 to 2035. 56
Yet over the five years to 2030 it sits well above the base case, which adds about 304,000 by then. 57

The business-events metric ties to the strategy's green-season focus. International association meetings
in Cape Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024 and are "primarily hosted between May and
November".58 That window takes in five of the six green-season months, April to September.59 Chapter 5
tracks the nearest indicators: international arrivals and association meetings in Cape Town (T1-01 and T2-
02).

Alignment with provincial strategy
The study's contract requires this report and its recommendations to align with two documents: the Growth
for Jobs Strategy (G4J) and Wesgro's FY26 to FY30 plan.60 This report is the Final Research Report
under clause 1.1.14, and Chapter 5 answers the monitoring and evaluation clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10
[clause references to be checked against the signed SLA Annexure B, whose wording is not in hand].

The Growth for Jobs Strategy 2023, approved by Cabinet, designates tourism a primary tradable sector
for the province.61 Its arrivals target is 3 million international tourist arrivals by 2035 on the South African
Tourism basis.62 [G4J 2023 document to be read and the 3 million target confirmed against it.] On that

basis the province received 1,538,142 in 2025.63 If the model counts on the same basis, that target sits

gates can be mapped to the plan cycle]. Yet the timing already fits. Chapter 4 sets its first decision gate at
the end of 2028, inside the plan period and before the base case crosses the model's seat line.

How the report counts arrivals
The arrivals figures around this strategy do not reconcile. Neither the 2.8 million ambition nor its later
restatement as 5.7 million arrivals and R54.7 billion of spend names a series or a base year. 65 Twice the
1.44 million international passenger arrivals at Cape Town International in FY24/25 is 2.88 million, close to
the first and far from the second.66 Eight figures now circulate and count four things: ambitions, airport
passengers, South African Tourism's tourist arrivals and the model's arrivals (Appendix A2). The model's
2025 base of 1,498,829 sits just below South African Tourism's count. Yet each market group's 2025
value tracks its 2024 value grown at the model's own rate. The report therefore treats the base as a model
value until its basis is confirmed.67 [Open item: Skift to confirm in writing what the model counts (SA
Tourism tourist arrivals, ACSA passengers including returning residents or OAG origin-and-destination
flows) and how the Hubs group is built.]

The report follows three rules. First, airport passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, the province's
gateway, are the baseline and tracking series, by fiscal year.68 FY24/25, at 1.44 million, is the recovery
baseline in section 1.2 and the baseline year in Chapter 5. Second, the model's 2025 base and its 2035
points are the target series. Third, South African Tourism's provincial count is used once, to read across to
the G4J target. The airport count for FY25/26, 1.57 million, shows why the rules matter. It sits above the
model's 2025 base and below its 2028 base-case point of 1,673,855.69 Yet the two differ in measure and
period, so the gap is no evidence that the model is cautious. The report quotes them side by side and
never adds one to the other [model owner to confirm the series and year basis behind the 2025 base of
1,498,829].


1.2 The Western Cape tourism system today

The Western Cape's international tourism has recovered to a record, and the system that
delivered the recovery runs through five source markets, six summer months and one metro.

Arrivals and recovery
International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in FY24/25, against 1.16m in
FY19/20, the last pre-pandemic year.70 Arrivals passed the pre-pandemic peak of 1.19m, set in FY18/19,
during FY23/24, when they reached 1.36m.71 FY25/26 added a further step to 1.57m.72

The fall and the climb were both steep. Arrivals dropped to 80,400 in FY20/21 and 378,500 in FY21/22,
then returned to 1.07m in FY22/23.73 Two-way international passengers reached 2.90m in FY24/25, 29%
above FY17/18,74 and 3.3m in calendar 2025 inside a total of 11.1m, both up 7% on the year.75

Cape Town's recovery also ran ahead of the national one, so the destination gained share as it recovered.
In the first half of 2024 its air arrivals had recovered further against 2019 than South Africa's in eight of the
ten largest markets.76 The USA stood at 162% and Australia at 172% of their 2019 level.77

Spend and value
Foreign direct spend in the province rose from R22.7bn in FY2024 to R25.9bn in FY2025, a gain of 14% in
one year.81 The Western Cape earns that spend from a modest share of the national market. Across the
four quarters of 2025 it took 16%, 13%, 13% and 16% of South Africa's foreign arrivals and 26%, 20%,
22% and 27% of foreign direct spend.82

The gap between the two shares is the province's premium. About a quarter of national spend from about
a seventh of arrivals means the average foreign visitor to the Western Cape spends roughly 1.6 times the
national average.83 The province earns about US$890 per international arrival, against US$500 for the rest
of South Africa and US$630 for Morocco, though below Kenya's US$1,460.84

More than a quarter of overseas visitors stay between 7 and 21 days, on printed bands that sum to only
48%.85 Some 5% stay 1 to 2 days, 12% 3 to 4, 14% 7 to 10, 3% 11 to 14, 13% 15 to 21 and 1% other. 86
The Diagnostic prints the same distribution from the 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey, in which
52.33% of respondents gave no length of stay, so the bands describe about half the sample. 87

The modal visitor in the 2024 Wesgro survey spends R1,000 to R2,000 a day, is aged 36 to 50 and travels
as a pair.88 Nearly 40% spend more than R1,000 a day.89 Self-catering at 17.4% and guesthouses at 9.7%
lead accommodation choice, ahead of hotels at 3.6%;90 together these shares cover under a third of
respondents.

Source markets
Five markets supply 63% of overseas arrivals. In 2025 the UK held 19%, Germany 17%, the USA 16%,
the Netherlands 7% and France 4%, and the three largest alone held 52%.91 The report uses 63%, the
sum of the printed shares.

Concentration is not new. On the Cape Town overseas air-arrivals basis for January to August 2024 the
same five markets held 61%, with the UK and the USA at 18% each and Germany at 15%. 92 The growth
markets remain small: India supplied about 2.6% of South Africa's overseas tourists in September 2025,
the Middle East 2.3% and China 1.4%.93

The mature markets also pay the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA,
R26,500 for the UK and R25,600 for Germany down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe. 94
The spread is 3.9 times, and the two regional markets compensate with stability, repeat demand and
counter-seasonal volume.95

Most of these visitors arrive through someone else's hub. The UAE route carried 222,923 air arrivals into
Cape Town in 2025, the UK 158,448, Qatar 126,078, Ethiopia 123,250 and Turkiye 89,090. 96 The four hub
routes through Dubai, Doha, Addis Ababa and Istanbul carried 53% of arrivals on the ten largest routes, 97
so the hubs, not the source markets, are the province's working gateways.

Seasonality
Demand peaks in the southern summer. In 2025, 12.7% of overseas visitors travelled in December, 11.5%
in January and 11.3% in November, against 6.1% in June and 6.5% in August.98 October to March
together account for about 62% of the year once the twelve printed shares, which sum to 104.9%, are
scaled to 100% [Wesgro Research to confirm the 2025 month-of-travel shares].

The 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey, whose monthly shares sum to 100%, gives 61% for October to
March and 39% for April to September.99

Supply follows the same curve. June carries 6.4% of 2025 international seat capacity into Cape Town, 100
and Cape Town hotel occupancy was 53% in June 2023.101 [Open item: Wesgro Research to supply June
2025 occupancy.] The Diagnostic drew the conclusion early: growth "will depend on improving conversion
in Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand into already busy peak months".102

Air and sea access
Ten airlines fly to Cape Town International from Africa, nine from Europe, three from North and South
America, two from the Middle East and one from Asia.103 The aviation assessment states the gap plainly
as "the absence of any direct capacity to any of the Asian markets", India and China included. 104 Twenty-
nine international routes serve the airport in 2026.105

Cape Town International itself is now the physical ceiling. One runway with no parallel taxiway caps
movements at about 30 an hour, and ACSA's programme of about R11.3bn brings relief from about 2032
for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway.106 ACSA's latest schedule shows the
realigned runway from December 2026 to December 2029, subject to contract award.107 The secondary
gateways do not change the picture: George, above its design limit at 911,000 passengers in 2025,
doubles by 2028 or 2029, with construction from October 2026,108 and Cape Winelands Airport targets
2028 with no named carrier.109

Cruise has grown without new berth capacity. The province hosted 70, 67 and 79 vessels in the 2022/23,
2023/24 and 2024/25 seasons, carrying 144,125, 109,884 and 127,178 passengers.110 The Infrastructure
Report counts 83 calls at the Cape Town berth in 2024/25, 127,000 passengers and R1.79bn of economic
impact.111 [StratEcon 2024/25 cruise report to confirm whether 79 vessels and 83 calls differ by definition.]

Port reliability, not berth space, is the risk. The single berth ran at about 40% of a season capacity of 150
or more calls, and its limits are a 300m ship length and the South-Easter wind.112 The Diagnostic calls the
Port of Cape Town "a binding constraint, with congestion and equipment failures affecting export and
cruise reliability".113

Geography of the system
The Cape Metro generates 72.5% of provincial GDPR, a whole-economy share rather than a tourism
one.114 The Cape Winelands follows at 11.2%, the Garden Route at 7.6%, the West Coast at 4.8%, the
Overberg at 3% and the Central Karoo at 0.5%.115 The decks date only the Garden Route share, to 2023,
while the Metro chart is titled 2024 and the rest carry no year.116

Tourism supply is concentrated in the same way. The Infrastructure Report mapped 50,332 tourism assets
across the six regions, of which the Metro holds 20,281, or 40.3%.117 The Garden Route holds 9,875, the
Cape Winelands 7,730, the Overberg 6,176, the West Coast 4,752 and the Central Karoo 1,518. 118

Cape Town is the axle of the system, "the province's global shopfront and primary dispersal hub" in the
Infrastructure Report's words.119 The Diagnostic warns that "any capacity failure in Cape Town quickly
becomes a provincial risk, not just a city problem".120 International visitors land there and disperse from
there, and readiness falls with each step away: accessibility scores drop from 4.7 in the Metro to 1.0 in the
Central Karoo.121

Beyond the Metro the visitor drives. The N2 is "the only continuous inter-regional tourism corridor, creating
a single point of failure", and large parts of the province outside the Metro and the Garden Route remain
effectively car-only destinations.122 The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls away.123

Rail, roads and utilities
Passenger rail has collapsed as a mode. Trainsets fell from about 110 in 1995 to about 91, pushing some
400,000 commuters onto saturated roads, and congestion in the Metro costs an estimated R2.8bn a
year.124 Metrorail still runs 460km, five lines and 104 of 121 stations, with 22.7m passenger trips in
FY2025, but no line reaches the airport and it is rated functionally unusable for tourism. 125

Utilities cap the periphery before demand does. Water scarcity in high-growth nodes such as the Garden
Route acts as a "hard ceiling" for visitor load, and load shedding raises costs and degrades the
experience in peripheral districts.126 The airport has no fuel pipeline and depends on road tankers,127 and
the 2017 to 2018 water crisis left a lasting perception of resource pressure.128

The remedies are named but not scheduled. The Diagnostic lists four transport enablers, none dated or
costed: the Cape Town Integrator, rail restoration and devolution, a high-speed city to airport link and
George to Cape Winelands passenger rail.129 PRASA's R21.1bn programme and the City's R123bn, 30-
year devolution case cover fleet and lines, and neither carries a tourism product or an airport link. 130

Institutions
Wesgro runs the demand side of this system through six units: Cape Town Air Access, Cruise Cape
Town, the Convention Bureau, Destination Marketing, Research and Tourism.131 Around them sit fifteen
external organisations, from national agencies and state-owned entities to the province, the City,
destination and attraction bodies and private operators.132

The levers that bind the system sit at different tiers. National government holds visa rules, border
compliance, air service agreements and route rights.133 The province holds jobs, inclusion and spatial
outcomes, sector development and infrastructure planning; local government holds service delivery,
precinct management and events readiness; industry holds route viability, demand generation and product
standards.134

Alignment across those tiers is thin. Stakeholders described strain at SA Tourism and a lack of fiscal
space.135 More than twenty bodies produce tourism data and "there is no single view of visitor behaviour,
making strategy reactive rather than predictive".136

Stakeholders say the same. Fifteen of the sixteen organisations interviewed raised industry and
government collaboration.137 The Department of Economic Development named restrictive bilateral air
agreements and destination funding materially below peer destinations as high-impact constraints. 138

Wesgro's six units sit inside a system of fifteen external organisations whose decision rights are split
across three spheres of government and industry.139 The six units are Cape Town Air Access, Cruise
Cape Town, the Convention Bureau, Destination Marketing, Research and Tourism. Between them they
cover route development, cruise, business events bids, destination marketing, research and tourism
promotion for Cape Town and the Western Cape.

The Western Cape Government (Economic Development and Tourism) and the City of Cape Town make
up provincial and city government. The provincial tier sets jobs, inclusion and spatial outcomes, runs
sector development and safety programmes and plans infrastructure and resilience. The local tier delivers
services and manages precincts. It also readies events and place experience and handles congestion, by-
laws and community impact.

South African Tourism, Airports Company South Africa, Transnet and the South African Maritime Safety
Authority sit at the national tier. It holds visa rules and border compliance. It also holds air service
agreements and route rights, sets national brand and investment priorities and runs airport capacity and
port operations.

Cape Town Tourism, the V&A Waterfront, CapeNature, South African National Parks and the Table
Mountain Aerial Cableway are the destination and attraction bodies. They carry demand generation and
destination marketing as well as product quality and visitor experience at the flagship sites. The private
operators, MSC Cruises, Cape Grace, Pullman Hotels and Resorts (Cape Town City Centre) and
Southern Sun, carry route and product viability and commercial performance. They also set pricing and
service standards. Together they form the industry tier.

None of these constraints is a shortage of visitors. The runway, the single corridor, a rail network visitors
cannot use, the thin winter schedule and decision rights split across three tiers all sit outside a marketing
budget. The system has recovered; its constraints are now physical and institutional.


1.3 From recovery to growth

The recovery is complete; gateway capacity caps growth in the summer peak, and demand
and conversion cap it in the green season.

The limits of recovery logic
The study set out in December 2025 to move the destination from recovery to growth; the hypothesis
tree's own framing reads that "the Western Cape must transition from recovery to growth". 140 The first half
of that task is done. International passenger arrivals at Cape Town International reached 1.44m in
FY24/25 against 1.16m in FY19/20, and 1.57m in FY25/26.141

Recovery logic assumed capacity waiting to be refilled. Arrivals fell to 80,400 in FY20/21,142 and the task
was to refill idle seats, rooms and routes. That slack is gone. South Africa's international seat capacity is
back at pre-pandemic levels, and Cape Town International runs the highest average load factor of the
country's five largest airports, 79% in 2025.143

Cape Town did more than refill its seats. It added 17 mainline international routes between 2015 and
2025, more than any of eight peer airports, and MIDAS calls the result "a 'what next' moment". 144 The
rebound that a recovery plan is built to capture has already happened.

Seats in summer, demand in winter
Growth is now supply-constrained in the summer peak and demand-constrained in the green season,
when airlines withdraw seats: June carries 6.4% of the year's international capacity.145 Skift's survey of 34
outbound markets counts 97 million high-value, South Africa-interested travellers, of whom only 1.4 million
convert into arrivals.146 The report's conclusion is that "the challenge is not demand generation but
improving conversion into actual visits".147

The binding supply is peak capacity at the gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line
at 1.8 million inbound seats, and the base case, in which Wesgro maintains current activity, reaches
1,802,929 arrivals at the 2030 plotted year.148 The chart below sets the base case against that line.

Figure 1.4. Base case against the seat line
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p20 (base case by year from a 2025 base of 1,498,829; the 2028 to 2035 points are identical
in the Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21). Seat line as drawn on the same slide. Airport relief from about 2032: Infrastructure Report, Jul
2026, p103. The model prints only these six year points and no seat table beneath the 1.8m line, so the crossing falls between 2028
(1,673,855) and 2030 (1,802,929). The conservative (1,822,634) and accelerated (2,516,282) cases are printed as 2035 end points only and
are not plotted.

The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap. At Cape Town International's 79% average load
factor, 1.8 million inbound seats would carry about 1.42 million arrivals, fewer than the 1.57 million ACSA
recorded in FY25/26.149 MIDAS tables 3,816,797 international seats for the year to November 2025, more
than 1.8 million each way even if that total counts both directions.150 What binds is the runway at peak
hours in the summer peak, at about 30 movements an hour.151 [Open item: ACSA or OAG to supply peak-
hour and seasonal slot headroom at Cape Town International.]

The routes that exist are full. Eleven of the 27 international routes in the load-factor table ran above 80%
in the year to November 2025, and the network averaged 76.0% on the printed total. 152 MIDAS tables 27
routes for the year to November 2025; 29 serve the airport in 2026. Five of the eleven are mature long-
haul routes, led by Newark at 88.6%, Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London Heathrow at 83.0%. 153

Full routes do not add seats of their own accord. MIDAS expects that "rather than increase capacity
carriers will seek to increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services".154 Airlines
also pull seats out of winter: June carries 6.4% of the year's international capacity and December
10.8%.155

Behind the routes sits the runway. "The airport, not a shortage of visitors is what now caps Cape Town's
tourism growth".156 One runway with no parallel taxiway caps movements at about 30 an hour, and
ACSA's programme of about R11.3bn brings relief from about 2032 for the terminal and about 2033 for the
runway.157

The alternatives do not close the gap in time. George, domestic only, doubles by 2028 or 2029, and Cape
Winelands Airport targets 2028 with no named carrier. In the Infrastructure Report's words, "the certain fix
is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is the least certain".158 Route rights sit a tier up: air service
agreements are a national function.159 The Department of Economic Development rates restrictive bilateral
agreements a high-impact constraint on airlift.160

Car-only regions and unbookable product
Beyond the Metro the constraint changes shape, not nature. Accessibility scores fall from 4.7 in the Cape
Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo.161 Large parts of the province outside the Metro and the Garden Route
remain effectively car-only destinations.162 The further from Cape Town, the faster the infrastructure falls
away.163

Much of what the car can reach cannot be booked. Most rural properties have no OTA presence and
Route 62 has the lowest online bookability of the six touring routes.164 Even CapeNature takes about 70%
of its reservations through a call centre rather than its online platform.165

Growth above the norm
Global tourism grows 3 to 5% a year under stable conditions, the band the Benchmarking Report calls
organic growth.166 Five of the six comparator destinations grew faster.167 Chile grew 40.4%, Singapore
21%, Kenya 14.6%, Valencia 13 to 14% and New Zealand about 12%, against global growth of 11% in
2024.168 New Zealand's figure matches its 2025 holiday-arrivals growth rather than the 5.9% for total
arrivals in 2025.169 Melbourne's 4.3% is an Australia-wide forecast rather than an actual,170 so the
comparator range runs from about 4% to 40%.

The Western Cape's two paths straddle the band. The base case implies 3.8% a year from 2025 to 2035
and the accelerated case 5.3%, and the accelerated case reverts to base "without confirmed routes". 171
The Benchmarking Report counts growth as structural only above that band. It must also be "driven by
deliberate changes to demand drivers, access conditions or product systems, rather than cyclical recovery
or short-term promotion".172

Cape Town is the axle of the system: international visitors arrive there and disperse from there, and "Cape
Town remains the engine of the system, but growth cannot keep concentrating there".173 The Department
of Economic Development already reads the growth framing as overweight on volumes and asks for a
value and spend lens in place of arrivals.174 Growth now needs new markets, new seasons and new
regions, and each of those is a supply problem before it is a marketing problem.

1.4 Approach and method

The study ran in four phases from November 2025, tested one hypothesis tree against every
interim deliverable and took each figure from a ranked hierarchy of sources.

Four phases
The Inception Report set out four phases with printed durations: inception, one month; demand-side
analysis, four months; supply-side analysis, three months; enablement and final reporting, seven
months.175 The Phase 4 detail slide prints six months for the same phase;176 the report uses seven, which
matches the Phase 4 bar on the deliverables gantt, May to the end of November. 177 The deliverables gantt
runs November to November without printing years.178

Each phase had a distinct job.179 Inception aligned expectations and ways of working and produced the
Current State Diagnostic as a literature-based baseline.180 The demand phase covered source markets,
India and China route to market, benchmarks and trends; the supply phase covered spatial readiness and
community inclusion, and the two overlapped from February to April.181

The final phase built the M&E framework, consolidated the strategy into this report and prepared the
launch.182 Three mechanisms governed the work: fortnightly working sessions with Wesgro, Steering
Committee sessions at milestones and fortnightly written status updates.183 The session calendar ran from
the kick-off on 13 November 2025 to a final-report Steerco pencilled for 12 June 2026.184

The hypothesis tree
The analytical core is a hypothesis tree agreed in December 2025 and tested by every workstream since.
The Current State Diagnostic printed it with three pillars, six themes and eighteen entries. 185 The pillars are
Reshape Demand, Transform Supply and Build Enabling Capability, each carrying two themes.

The tree disciplined the evidence. The Stakeholder Insights Report mapped all 223 of its insight
statements to tree entries before aggregating them.186 Because the study team mapped the insights to tree
entries, the counts show where views cluster; they do not on their own validate an entry. The later
deliverables reprinted the tree as a recap and marked the branch they addressed.

Three entries changed wording between December 2025 and March 2026.187 The seasonality entry gained
mega events; the institutional entry widened from Wesgro, DEDAT and CTT to local, provincial and
national; the collaboration entry added public and private. No document in hand dates the change more
closely than the 23 January working session and the 6 February Steerco.188

The 14 October 2026 review updated the tree to twenty focus areas and gave each a disposition
[Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck not in hand; page citation to confirm]. The three
pillars and six themes stand. The Infrastructure Report of 22 September still carried the eighteen-entry
tree, so the move to twenty belongs to the final review rather than to any one workstream. 189

The entries moved in three ways; first, the air access and cruise entry split in two, because the aviation
assessment and the Infrastructure Report showed them to be different problems. Second, the
infrastructure, visa and safety entry moved from the supply pillar to governance, because its levers sit with
national government. Third, a workforce and service skills entry was added and flagged, because the
300,000-jobs ambition has no labour analysis behind it.190

Six further entries were reworded to the lever the evidence supports and one was refined; the table below
lists each change and the workstream behind it. Ten entries carried through as Validated with title-only
edits. Chapter 3 records every disposition and section 1.5 defines the labels [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct
2026, slide 14: the deck's own change count and label definitions to confirm].

Source hierarchy
Every figure in this report carries a source and page, and sources rank in five tiers. Where tiers disagree
the higher tier wins; where two documents in one tier disagree, both values are recorded and the value
used is stated with the reason. Nothing is reconciled by averaging.

         prints page numbers.

Two conventions follow. The MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment and the Skift memos are Word files
without page numbers, so they are cited by section title. The 14 October 2026 summary deck ranks below
the deliverables: where the two differ, the deliverable's figure is used and the difference is logged.

Interim deliverables
Nine dated documents carry the evidence. Appendix A9 dates each by its cover and gives the version in
hand and the SLA clauses it answers. Chapter 2 draws them together as one body of analysis in four
parts: the global benchmarks and their lessons (2.1), demand (2.2), supply (2.3) and the enabling
environment (2.4). The Inception Report frames 1.1 and 1.4, and the Current State Diagnostic frames 1.2.
The Market Assessment Report of May 2026 is the working analysis behind the Demand-Side Analysis
and alone prints the 2025 base the growth case uses.193

Stakeholder engagement
Stakeholder engagement ran in four phases alongside the analysis.194 Introduction and framing ran from
January to March 2026, sense-making and validation in April and May, strategy socialisation in June and
July and mobilisation from August into October. The Stakeholder Insights Report closed the first phase
with insight tables for sixteen organisations, from Mossel Bay to the CTICC. 195 Sixteen is a count of those
tables; the report prints no tally.

The method was strict about what an insight is. Sessions were run, insights extracted, each mapped to a
tree entry and then aggregated.196 The 223 statements are the project team's paraphrases rated High,
Medium or Low, not quotations, so this report attributes them to organisations and never to named
individuals.197

Two gaps in the record matter: phase 1 engaged no airline, online travel agent or tour operator, and the
six demand-side engagements were proposed for phase 2.198 [Open item: test the gateway and
governance chapters with ACSA, the Cape Town Air Access airline partners, the City of Cape Town and
Cape Town Tourism before Steerco.] The demand work drew instead on discussions with more than 20
stakeholders of its own.199 The outcomes of engagement phases 2 to 4 sit in no document in hand
[Letsema to supply the phase 2 to 4 engagement record for Appendix A6].

Data limits
Five limits in the data shape how the findings read. The headline arrivals series counts international
passenger arrivals at Cape Town International, not tourists, and Cape Town air arrivals stand proxy for the
province in the market shares.200 No approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs,
and no deliverable prints a Western Cape tourism employment baseline.201

The supply dataset behind the Infrastructure Report (Supply_Infrastructure.xlsx) holds no geography
below district, and its 17,195 records added after May 2026 are generated placeholders, as the study
team found when it checked the file. [Open item: confirm whether the Infrastructure Report's count of
50,332 assets includes those records; if it does, restate Tables 1.2, 2.3 and A.18 and the district shares
drawn from them.] The inclusion analysis runs on an OpenStreetMap asset layer, extracted on 16
September 2026, joined to Census 2011 sub-places, so its resident shares are fifteen years old. 202

The growth model exists in two editions. The May Market Assessment runs from a 2025 base of
1,498,829 arrivals and the June Demand-Side Analysis from a 2024 base of 1,445,031.203 Both reach
2,175,778 in the base case and 2,516,282 in the accelerated case by 2035. The report runs the growth
case from the 2025 base, keeps FY24/25's 1.44m as the recovery baseline and states which is which
wherever both appear.

Completion plan
A six-week completion plan agreed on 12 October 2026 governs the last stage of the work. [Summary of
Findings, 14 Oct 2026: six-week plan from 12 October, with weekly outputs and owners, to be inserted;
deck not in hand.] It replaces the inception timetable, which placed report consolidation between 1 June
and 27 July 2026 and launch support to 2 November.204 The report goes to Steerco, Exco and the Board
before the public launch in February 2027.


1.5 How to read this report

The report is built so that a Board member can read the summary and any one focus area
on its own, and a programme owner can run the plan from Chapters 3 to 5.

Chapter map
The report moves from context to evidence to recommendations to delivery.

The executive summary tells the case in short: where the province stands, what caps growth and what 2.5
million arrivals would take. It then sets out the three shifts and the twenty focus areas, the first 24 months,
the headline indicators, the main risks and the Board's decisions. It stands alone, for the Board, Exco and
the February 2027 launch audience. Chapter 1 is for every reader, and is the chapter of the body a Board
member should read if they read no other. It sets out what Wesgro asked for, where the system stands
after recovery, why the next step is constrained by peak capacity and how the study ran.

Chapter 2 sets out what the analysis found, in four parts: the global benchmarks and their lessons,
demand, supply and the enabling environment. It serves readers checking the evidence behind a Chapter
3 entry, and each part closes with a box naming the focus areas it supports. Chapter 3 takes the twenty
focus areas in turn: the December hypothesis, its disposition, the evidence, what Wesgro does, what it

Disposition labels
Each Chapter 3 entry records what the study believed in December 2025 and what the evidence did to
that belief. The December tree held eighteen entries under three pillars and six themes; 205 the tree settled
at the 14 October 2026 review keeps the pillars and themes and holds twenty focus areas.

Seven labels record what the evidence did to each December hypothesis, and ten of the twenty focus
areas carry Validated: the evidence confirmed the hypothesis as written. One is Refined, where the
evidence confirmed the hypothesis and sharpened its content. Six are Reworded: the evidence confirmed
the hypothesis and the entry was renamed to what the evidence showed. One is New, added in October
2026 with no December entry behind it. One is Moved: confirmed, but placed in another theme because
its levers sit with another tier. One is Flagged: untested, and carried as a question for analysis still to be
commissioned. None is Withdrawn, the label for an entry dropped because the evidence did not support it.
The labels are as recorded at the 14 October 2026 review [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to
16: deck page reference to be confirmed against the final deck], and the counts come from the traceability
table in Appendix A1.

What the evidence argued against. No entry was withdrawn, but the evidence narrowed or overturned
three parts of the December case. First, the pivot to four growth markets narrowed to converting India and
China traffic already flying through hubs; the Middle East and ASEAN cases did not survive (focus area 4).
Second, the ambition to double arrivals did not survive: the growth model supports 2.2 million to 2.5 million
by 2035 (section 1.1). Third, the 300,000 tourism-linked jobs ambition has no baseline behind it and is
flagged untested (focus area 20). Two Validated entries also rest on less than direct evidence: focus area
5 has no fare or yield data, and focus area 15 is an inference from the pipeline tables (section 3.3).

The counts sum to twenty. Lead removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits carries Moved: it keeps
its December wording but moves from supply theme 2.1 to governance theme 3.2, because its levers sit
nationally. [Summary of Findings deck, 14 Oct 2026, to confirm the label for the moved entry and that
nothing was Withdrawn]

Every figure carries its source and page in a footnote, in the form Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21.
The date is the cover date and the page is the PDF page index counted from the cover, because none of
the decks prints page numbers. The MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment and the three Skift memos have

impact, T2-01 for outcome and T3-01 for output.

Chapter 2


Analysis and insights
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                             DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Four parts build the case for the strategy in Chapter 3. Section 2.1 draws lessons from comparator
destinations; sections 2.2, 2.3 and 2.4 then examine demand, supply and the enabling environment, on
which Pillars 1, 2 and 3 rest.

Demand is not what holds the province back. Its shape and the system that serves it do. Five markets and
six months carry most overseas visits, and seats into Cape Town run out at the peak. Beyond the Metro,
product is hard to reach and book, and in townships and rural settlements the missing piece is a local
seller. The institutions that should hold the system together have thinned. Section 2.1 first shows how the
fastest growers dealt with their own binding constraint.

Stakeholder evidence runs through sections 2.2 to 2.4. The first of four engagement phases ran from
January to March 2026.211 In it, 16 organisations from Mossel Bay to the CTICC gave 223 rated insights:
115 High, 102 Medium and 6 Low.212 Each stakeholder statement in this chapter is the project team's
paraphrase of a point an organisation made, attributed to that organisation and never to a person. [Open
item: each organisation named here to approve its paraphrase before publication.] Each part ends with a
box, "What this means for the strategy". The box says what the evidence does to the focus areas it
touches: it validates, rewords, refines or supports them, or supplies precedent or evidence.


2.1 Global benchmarks and their lessons

The destinations that grew fastest ran institutions, marketing and air access as one system
aimed at a single binding constraint. For the Western Cape that constraint is seats into one
gateway; in its townships and rural settlements it is the lack of a local seller.

The six comparators are Chile; Melbourne and Victoria; Valencia; Singapore; Kenya; and Auckland and
New Zealand.213 They came from a screen of 30 destinations against 12 criteria in four groups: market and
access; destination and product structure; growth and inclusion; performance.214 Each case was read for
its demand profile, two proven growth levers and how far they transfer to the Western Cape. 215 Growth of
3% to 5% a year is the norm under stable conditions; above 5% marks structural acceleration and 10% or
more a structural shift.216 UN Tourism puts global growth at 11% in 2024, while recovery was still running,
and about 4% in 2025.217 Growth counts as structural only when it is "driven by deliberate changes to
demand drivers, access conditions or product systems, rather than cyclical recovery or short-term
promotion".218

Only two comparators grew well above the world
Only two of the six comparators grew well above the world in 2024. Chile grew 40.4% and Singapore
21%, two to four times the world's rate.219 Kenya (14.6%), Valencia (13% to 14%) and New Zealand (about
12%) grew only slightly faster. Melbourne's 4.3% is an Australia-wide forecast of average annual growth
rather than an actual.220

Cape Town wins more routes than its peers
Cape Town wins routes faster than its peers. Between 2015 and 2025 it added 17 mainline international
services, more than any of eight secondary airports chosen for peripheral geography and inbound
tourism.222 The eight are Calgary, Christchurch, Edinburgh, Keflavik, Larnaca, Marrakech, Santiago and
Valencia. Santiago added 14 services, Valencia 12 and Calgary 8.223 Yet winning routes is not the same
as holding seats through the winter, and the routes won now run full (section 2.3). The Western Cape
does not need to learn how to win routes. It needs what the leaders added: access funded as
infrastructure and marketing tied to each route.

The leaders treat air access as infrastructure
Chile, the fastest grower, put access first and treated it as infrastructure. Santiago handles about 90% to
95% of Chile's 5.2 million international arrivals. Its airport traffic rose from about 16 million passengers in
2010 to more than 26 million in 2019.224 A private concession won in 2015 funded about US$900 million of
expansion, under one of the world's most liberal aviation systems built on open-skies agreements. 225

New Zealand made marketing money follow routes. It "shifted from awareness-driven marketing to route
accountable demand generation", tying budgets to specific long-haul corridors and designing campaigns
around airline breakeven and shoulder-season loads.226 The programme reports more than 16,000
incremental bookings and a return of about NZ$55 million in 2023/24. Government-backed early-stage
route incentives and data shared between tourism bodies and airlines support it.227

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                          DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



For the Western Cape, the two cases point to three changes. Air access should be treated as "core
economic infrastructure, not a tourism add-on", with "a permanent, well-funded Public-Private Air Access
Fund".228 The province should "actively lobby for air service liberalisation and simplified visa regimes".229
Marketing budgets should be ring-fenced around priority long-haul corridors, with an aviation partnership
framework and early-stage route support.230

Transit and events can be turned into demand
Hubs can be converted into visits, not just flown through. Singapore does it through its stopover
programme, with the tourism board, Changi Airport Group and Singapore Airlines working "under a
coordinated strategy".231 It received 16.5 million international visitors in 2024 and record tourism receipts
of about S$29 billion.232 Kenya positioned Nairobi as East Africa's aviation, diplomatic and business hub,
and its main airport now carries 68% of arrivals.233

Events do the same work across the calendar. Victoria curates a portfolio of events that "generate repeat,
predictable demand spikes across the year".234 Melbourne "uses its event calendar to de-risk and
stimulate aviation demand".235 Behind the calendar, hotel stock grew 21%, about 5,000 rooms.236

The province needs one event acquisition and calendar strategy across Wesgro, the Convention Bureau,
the City and the Province, with conference bidding aligned to sector strengths.237 MICE should be treated
as a system lever, with city, provincial and national actors aligned around a gateway strategy. 238
Government, aviation, events and industry should work to shared KPIs.239 The Western Cape already has
the hub traffic and the green-season gap that these levers address (section 2.2).

Quality and value are governed, not encouraged
The leaders manage quality and yield as deliberate outcomes. Valencia adopted a destination-wide quality
system across its tourism businesses and placed coordination in "a permanent public-private structure led
by Visit València".240 It received 3.5 million international visitors in 2024, 13.9% more than in 2023 and
24.4% above 2019.241

New Zealand manages regional capacity to protect pricing and recommends that destinations "replace
arrival growth with yield-based regional performance metrics".242 Kenya earned about US$1,460 per visitor
in 2024 by building conservation and community participation into one governed product system.243

For the Western Cape the cases ask first for a province-wide quality and standards framework led by
Wesgro.244 Within it would sit a provincial quality charter phased by district, with certification "visible across
booking platforms".245 Standing sector councils for wine, MICE, adventure and culture would sit beside the
charter.246 The cases also ask the province to measure its regions by yield rather than arrivals and to build

conservation and community inclusion into the offer.247 Quality, price and yield are managed outcomes,
not by-products of volume.

In communities, lasting sellers matter more than capital
In communities the binding constraint is the seller. Five further cases, from Rio de Janeiro to Kliptown,
show that local sellers outlast the programmes that launch them.248 In Santa Marta in Rio de Janeiro,
about 20 resident guides ran the tours by 2015 and outside jeep firms had left. The guides kept selling
after the launch programme ended, while assets and training without a route to market did not last. 249
Access and safety were preconditions, not product.

Scale brings its own limits. Medellín's Comuna 13 drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025, 52% of them
international, with about 300 professional guides. Mobility built for residents became the stage, but
carrying-capacity limits came late.250 On Vilakazi Street in Soweto, ten restaurants employ more than 800
people. Public space upgrades amplify existing assets, but benefits concentrate on street-front owners. 251

Neither capital nor donor money lasts without a seller that can stand alone. Costa Rica's community
network turned over more than USD 800,000 in 2013 and dissolved at the end of 2018, after donors left. 252
A selling entity is the engine and must survive donor exit. Kliptown in Soweto received a first allocation of
R436 million and was projected to draw almost 200,000 international visitors a year. By 2017 its museum
drew about 4,000 visitors a year, because capital built before an operator yields no operating income. 253

The cases converge on one order: "a seller with a stake, a safe and workable visit, product that keeps
spend in the community, then exposure".254 In the Western Cape's communities, the seller therefore
comes before the asset (section 2.4).

The system matters more than any lever
No single lever explains the leaders' growth. Sustained tourism growth "is not created by fragmented
interventions, but by a coordinated destination system in which governance, demand generation and
access reinforce one another".255 Chile's coordination works best "when anchored around a single
strategic constraint", which for Chile was long-haul access.256 The Western Cape's constraint has the
same shape as Chile's: seats into one gateway airport (section 2.3).

Yet coordination is hard everywhere. Victoria reports tension between national and state bodies over roles
and branding.257 Valencia struggles to join its data across institutions, and Kenya's collaboration between
government, industry and financiers is "mixed".258 The Western Cape's three tiers of government show the
same strain (section 2.4).

The strategy should follow the same order: peak seats into Cape Town first, one system built around them
and, in the communities, a seller before any asset.

      Resident sellers outlasted the programmes that launched them, and capital built before an operator,
      as in Kliptown, earned no operating income.




2.2 Demand-side factors

Interest in the Western Cape is broad and growing. Yet demand is concentrated in five
markets and six months. The growth markets fly in through Gulf and African hubs and book
on platforms where Western Cape product is hard to find.

A 2025 values-based survey reached more than 25,000 travellers in 34 outbound markets, 17 of them
Wesgro priority markets, with 800 to 1,600 in each.259 More than 20 discussions with market-facing
stakeholders added judgement to desk review of Wesgro reports, Statistics South Africa and Google
search data.260

Five markets carry the province and pay most
The UK, Germany, the USA, the Netherlands and France supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and
the first three alone 52%.261 The pattern is not new: the top five held 61% of Cape Town overseas air
arrivals from January to August 2024.262

The same markets pay the most. Average spend per visitor in 2024 ran from R32,900 for the USA and
R26,500 for the UK down to R8,600 for Namibia and R8,500 for Zimbabwe.263 High-spend visitors come
from Europe and North America, "tying future value growth to slower-growing source markets". 264 The
Western Cape must therefore hold yield in its mature markets while volume growth comes from
elsewhere.

Six months carry three fifths of demand
Demand peaks in the southern summer and thins in winter. December, January and November took
12.7%, 11.5% and 11.3% of overseas visitors in 2025, while June fell to 6.1%.265 October to March hold
about 62% of the year.266 The 2024 Wesgro Visitor Tracking Survey gives a similar split of 61% and
39%.267

Growth therefore depends on "improving conversion in Apr-Sep rather than pushing incremental demand
into already busy peak months".268 Airlines cut seats in those same months, so filling the April to
September green season needs seats as well as conversion (section 2.3).

Removing barriers, not demand, sets the upside
The scenarios differ by intervention, not by demand: "the difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed".269 The growth model builds each
market's rate from four levers: outbound growth, the Western Cape's penetration, the airlift ceiling and
structural conditions such as currency and visas.270 It anchors on OAG aviation data for access and
traveller data for demand.271

From the 2025 base, the base case reaches 2,175,778 arrivals by 2035, implied growth of 3.8% a year.
The conservative case reaches 1,822,634, at 2.0%, and the accelerated case 2,516,282, at 5.3%. 272 The
base case's 3.8% sits within the 3% to 5% norm, and only the accelerated case clears the 5% mark of
structural acceleration (section 2.1).

The step from the base case's 2.18m to 2.52m rests on confirmed direct routes to India and China, above-
baseline activation and more hub capacity into Cape Town.273 The dependency is hard: "without confirmed
routes, volume reverts to base".274 Seats are the first such barrier: even the base case passes the model's
seat line at the 2030 year point (section 2.3).

Hub and acceleration markets add most arrivals
In the base case the hub markets add the most arrivals and the acceleration markets grow fastest. The
hub markets are the UAE, Ethiopia, Qatar and Turkey. The acceleration markets are the Gulf, China, India
and Brazil.275 Between 2025 and 2035 hub markets add 206,584 arrivals, mature markets 178,780,
acceleration markets 175,818, regional markets 110,298 and Japan and South Korea 5,469.276




Figure 2.3. Base-case growth by market group
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p20. Groups as printed: Mature (UK, Germany, USA, Netherlands, France, Switzerland);
Hubs (UAE, Ethiopia, Qatar, Turkey); Regional (Namibia, Zimbabwe, Zambia, Botswana, Kenya and others); Acceleration (Gulf, China, India,
Brazil); Strategic (Japan, South Korea).

India more than doubles from 76,547 to 165,260 arrivals and China with Hong Kong from 43,412 to
92,598, both at 8% a year.277 Spend follows the mix. The model fixes average spend for each group, from
R24,500 a visitor in mature markets to R8,500 in regional ones. On that basis base-case foreign spend
rises from a modelled R27.12bn in 2024 to R40.59bn in 2035.278 Volume and value come from different
markets.

The right segments can spread demand
Four values-based segments fit the Western Cape, together about 97 million addressable travellers. 279
Each has priority markets: Curious Explorers in the UK and the USA, and Creative Connectors in India.
Discerning Connoisseurs are paired with China and the UAE, and Reflective Adventurers with Germany. 280

Curious Explorers and Discerning Connoisseurs are "most aligned to the Western Cape's offering".281 The
segments also matter for spread, not only for volume. Curious Explorers offer the "strongest opportunity
for geographic dispersal beyond Cape Town"; Reflective Adventurers have "strong potential to support
seasonality mitigation" through year-round outdoor and wellness travel.282 These two are the segments to
target for the regions and the green season.

India and China already arrive through hubs
The growth markets are already in the air, on other carriers. About 71,000 Indian travellers and about
30,000 Chinese reach Cape Town each year through connecting hubs, together about 101,000
journeys.283 Addis Ababa and Dubai carry 81% of the Indian travellers, and half of them start in Mumbai. 284
Direct Indian service is "unlikely before 2030 due to aircraft constraints at Air India and IndiGo". 285 For
China, a China Southern service from Guangzhou is the likeliest first direct route.286

Australia (61,000 indirect travellers) and Saudi Arabia (137,000 passengers, with Riyadh Air's planned
hub) come next.287 The Western Cape should convert this hub traffic now, as Singapore converts transit
into visits (section 2.1). Wesgro should build early relationships with the Indian carriers and Indian
government authorities.288 It should treat China as a long-term investment in awareness and indirect
volume before any non-stop service.289

Each market books on its own platforms
Conversion happens on platforms that differ by market. Chinese travellers discover on Douyin and
Xiaohongshu and book on Trip.com, Qunar and Fliggy. Indian travellers book through MakeMyTrip,
Booking.com and Expedia, and much Gulf travel still runs through private advisors and concierge
agencies.290

Each market also has its own gap. Cape Town has no direct flight to Mumbai or Delhi and thin Indian-
friendly adaptation, while the Gulf lacks Arabic-language booking and local-currency pricing.291 Visas add
a further barrier, and they sit with national government (section 2.4).

The province's own channels also lag. The Department of Economic Development rates destination
platforms behind international practice in online presence and AI adoption. The Overberg says its
proximity to Cape Town is not packaged before travellers book flights.292 The money should therefore
follow the platforms: activate inside them and shift investment "toward trade-led conversion rather than
broad awareness campaigns".293

Africa is a base and a growth market
Closer to home, Africa already supplies a large and steady base. The province recorded 323,971 tourist
arrivals from 16 reported African markets in 2024, 62% of them from Zimbabwe and Namibia. 294 African
visitors spent R4.2bn in the province in 2025, and among them Nigerians and Ghanaians spent most per
visit, at R31,000 and R27,700.295 Namibia and Zimbabwe "add stability, repeat demand and counter-
seasonal volume".296

Nigeria, Kenya and Ghana are the priority growth markets, though the sources rank Ghana differently. 297
Nigeria's addressable market is modelled at 310,000 to 510,000 adult trips a year and Kenya's at 240,000
to 400,000.298 Africa is therefore both a regional foundation to keep and a source of high-spending visitors
to grow.

Business events are the strongest green-season lever
Business events run on a different calendar from leisure travel. International association meetings in Cape
Town rose from 33 in 2022 to 58 in 2024. Of the 136 held over the three years, 109 fell between May and
November.299 Cape Town ranked 35th in the 2024 ICCA ranking and hosted 37 of South Africa's 64 UIA-
recorded meetings in 2025.300 Hotel occupancy in Cape Town was 53% in June 2023, the gap such events
fill.301

Yet MICE is not one business. Meetings, incentives, conventions and exhibitions have different buyers
and lead times. Only conferences are measured well, and meetings are "absent from consolidated
provincial statistics".302 Stellenbosch and George are credible secondary hubs that can take delegates'
extra nights while major events stay in Cape Town.303 The lever needs one events calendar, as Melbourne
has (section 2.1), and measures for each of its four businesses.

Pillar 1 must fill the green season, win growth markets on the routes and platforms they already use, hold
yield in mature markets and build the African base. Seats, regional product and the enabling environment
(sections 2.3 and 2.4) decide whether that demand lands.

      What this means for the strategy
      Validates FA01, Respond to growth segment traveller preferences. Four segments, each with
      named priority markets.
      Supports FA02, Create green-season demand with MICE and events. Most international
      association meetings fall from May to November, a span that takes in the winter low. The seat side of
      the case sits in section 2.3.
      Supports FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Conversion runs through
      market-specific platforms that Western Cape product must reach.
      Rewords FA04, Convert India and China hub traffic. About 101,000 hub journeys a year and a
      direct Indian route unlikely before 2030.
      Validates FA05, Increase yield in mature markets. The highest-spending visitors come from the
      slowest-growing markets.
      Validates FA06, Build a regional foundation (Africa). A large regional base and two high-yield
      growth markets.




2.3 Supply-side factors

Supply sets the ceiling on growth. The gateway's routes run full, the new runway is years
away and airlines are expected to raise fares before they add seats. Beyond the Metro the
product exists but is hard to reach, book or stay in.

Stakeholders rate supply constraints the most severe: 44 of the 68 insights on supply are rated High,
against 23 of 64 on demand.304 Two bodies of evidence test that view. Scheduled seats and estimated
bookings cover every Cape Town route, with bookings from the airlines' global distribution systems to
November 2025.305 On the ground, 50,332 tourism assets are mapped across six regions in three layers:
attractions, services and enabling infrastructure. The map draws on Wesgro's supply database, SANParks
and CapeNature data and open geographic sources. Its premise is that "destination performance is
constrained by the weakest layer".306 [Dataset limits, including the absence of geography below district
level, to be confirmed against the supply dataset notes.] Each region is scored from 1 to 5 on
attractiveness (40%), readiness and capacity (30%) and accessibility (30%).307

The model hits its seat line by 2030
On the growth model, seats run out before demand does. The model draws current air access as "a hard
ceiling at 1.8 million inbound seats". The base case passes that line by 2030, at 1,802,929 arrivals, up
from 1,673,855 in 2028.308 The line is the model's assumption, not a measured cap; what binds in practice
is the runway at peak hours (section 1.3). On the model's own judgement, even 2.1 million arrivals by
2035, below the base case (section 2.2), needs deliberate intervention to add capacity. 309 Growth to 2.5
million is therefore a supply programme before it is a marketing one.
