Cape Town's routes already run full
Cape Town has won more routes than its peers (section 2.1), and they now run full. Twenty-nine
international routes serve the airport in 2026, and Cape Town's share of South Africa's international
capacity rose from 10% in 2010 to 25%.310 In the year to November 2025, 11 of the 27 routes with load
data ran above 80%. Newark led at 88.6%, ahead of Atlanta at 84.3% and Frankfurt and London
Heathrow at 83.0%.311 Sixteen ran at 75% or more.312




Figure 2.4. Route load factors to November 2025
Source: MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, "Seasonality" (international route load-factor table: estimated bookings divided by
capacity, bookings for the twelve months to November 2025). Routes above 80% highlighted; the eleven routes below 75% are not shown.

Cape Town's 79% average load factor is the joint highest of South Africa's five largest airports, with
Lanseria. South Africa averages about 70%, Africa 75% and the world 84%.313 Regional routes also run
full, and all five markets are named for more capacity. Luanda ran at 86.2% and 83.4% on its two airports,
Maputo 83.3%, Gaborone 82.3%, Mauritius 81.8% and Harare 81.3%. Domestically, Cape Town to
Durban runs at 90.0% and warrants more seats, while other domestic routes sit between 70% and 80%. 314

Cape Town has reached "a 'what next' moment". Near-term seats must come from more frequencies and
larger aircraft on routes already full. Beyond that, the province needs a route strategy that "looks for long-
term gains rather than short-term wins".315

The network runs through hubs and skips Asia
Cape Town's seven largest international markets are all hub airports, with 2.7 million seats in 2026 against
2.0 million in 2019. Western Europe holds 38% of international capacity, the Middle East 23%, Southern

Africa 17%, Eastern Africa 14% and North America 6%.316 Most hub passengers connect: Emirates'
connecting share is "above 80% annually" and Qatar Airways' is nine in ten.317

New carriers lean towards Africa and the hubs. Seven of the twelve that launched Cape Town services
since 2014 fly to African points. By 2026 seats the largest are Airlink to regional points (441,602) and
Ethiopian to Addis Ababa (437,430). Turkish to Istanbul (240,200), United to Newark and Washington
(172,190) and Kenya Airways to Nairobi (127,138) follow.318

Yet no seat flies direct to Asia: the network shows "the absence of any direct capacity to any of the Asian
markets".319 Only one airline serves Cape Town from Asia, against ten from Africa. 320 A direct Asian route
is therefore a long-term prize, and the near-term seats sit with the hub carriers.

Airlines cut winter seats and may raise fares
On full routes, airlines are expected to raise fares before they add seats. The forecast is that "carriers will
seek to increase revenues via higher selling fares rather than add new services", which "will frustrate that
desire for increased visitors".321 No fare data backs the forecast; it rests on judgement of airline behaviour.
In winter carriers withdraw seats. June carried 6.4% of Cape Town's 2025 international seats and
December 10.8%, with May, July, August and September at 6.9% each.322




Figure 2.5. International seats by month, 2025
Source: MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, "Seasonality" (Capetown international capacity by month, 2025, printed data labels).

Connecting traffic is "especially critical in the low season", which ties the winter schedule to the hub
carriers' own priorities.323 New seats must therefore be won route by route and season by season, as New
Zealand did with route-tied marketing (section 2.1).

One runway caps the gateway
The airport, not the airlines, sets the outer limit. Cape Town International's single runway has no parallel
taxiway and caps movements at about 30 an hour. Two of the airport's seven parts are already full. The
R6.39bn runway relocation lifts the cap to 45 movements an hour, a 50% rise. On the planning timeline it
completes around 2033, with terminal relief from about 2032.324 ACSA's latest schedule is earlier, with the
runway finished by December 2029, subject to contract award.325

The alternatives arrive sooner but carry less. George handled 911,000 passengers in 2025, above its
design limit. A R310m terminal expansion doubles its capacity to about 2 million passengers by 2028 or
2029. Cape Winelands Airport targets first passengers in 2028 on an R8bn first phase but has no firm
airline. Capacity and certainty pull apart: "the certain fix is the latest, and the earliest big-capacity option is
the least certain".326

Yet no stakeholder named Cape Town International's capacity as a constraint, and it appears only as a
transfer-time reference. No airline or airport sat among the 16 organisations, so the runway's absence
from their concerns is a gap in the record, not a finding.327 The outer limit is a national lever, held by ACSA
(section 2.4).

Cruise needs product, not berths
Cape Town's cruise berth has spare capacity, and port spending is not going to cruise. The berth is used
at about 40% of its season capacity of 150 or more calls. In 2024/25 it handled 83 calls and about 127,000
passengers, for R1.79bn of impact.328 Its limits are a 300m ship length, the South-Easter wind and no
shore power.329

Mossel Bay handled 21 calls and about 13,000 passengers and opened a R22m passenger facility in
November 2025. Ships anchor and tender because it has no cruise quay, and only small and mid-sized
ships call. Saldanha Bay has had 3 trial calls and shares the ore terminal, with no cruise berth or
passenger facility. Its R4bn port upgrade serves iron ore.330 Mossel Bay's own count differs: calls rose
from four before COVID to 27 a season.331

Mossel Bay is ready for day stops, so the stance is to protect Cape Town, back Mossel Bay now and
watch Saldanha Bay. The province should grow cruise "through product and marketing, not capital
works".332

The flagships are full in season
The province's best-known sites run at capacity in summer. At the Table Mountain Aerial Cableway, peak
queues of up to three hours up and two down detract from the visitor experience. 333 Chapman's Peak
suffers disruptions, and the December to January peak "overwhelms transport and accommodation
capacity".334 Boulders is "already near carrying capacity", and summit expansion on Table Mountain is
severely limited.335 Yet Cape Point and the township attractions are underused.336 Eleven of the 16

organisations see the offer as concentrated around Cape Town and a narrow set of icons.337 The peak

Readiness and access fall away from the Metro
Overall readiness halves between the Metro and the periphery. The Cape Metro rates 4.5 out of 5, the
Winelands and the Garden Route 3.4, the Overberg 2.7, the West Coast 2.5 and the Central Karoo 1.9. 338
Accessibility falls fastest. On attractions it scores 4.7 in the Metro, 3.3 in the Winelands and 3.0 on the
Garden Route. It drops to 2.0 in the Overberg, 1.7 on the West Coast and 1.0 in the Central Karoo, while
the Metro scores 5.0 on transport.339

Cape Town is both the gateway and the dispersal point. The Metro holds 20,281 of the 50,332 assets

strengths and weaknesses p88 to p90.

Beyond the Metro and the Garden Route the visitor drives. Public transport is "effectively absent" on the
West Coast and in the Overberg, and the Winelands have no transit between estates or valleys. Every
accessibility measure in the Central Karoo "sits at the floor", and even Metro rail is "functionally unusable
for tourism".342 Stakeholders call weak public transport, congestion and unreliable last-mile access
"structural barriers to visitor movement within and beyond Cape Town".343 Dispersal therefore "needs
deliberate corridor investment, not organic spillover from Cape Town".344

Strong regional assets lack beds and booking
The assets that could draw visitors outward sit where few can stay or book. The West Coast has the
province's highest natural-asset attractiveness and the Central Karoo its most unique niche assets, yet
both score lowest on readiness. Inland areas north of the N2 on the Garden Route are tourism-inactive.
Industrial belts in the Metro show "zero tourism conversion" despite nearby attractions. 345

Beds are one gap. In the Winelands mid-market accommodation "is effectively absent outside
Stellenbosch", and a "luxury-skewed product mix excludes mid-market and family segments".
Accommodation is thin beyond Langebaan and Paternoster on the West Coast and thin at international
grade along Route 62.346 No bed count yet sizes the gap.

Booking is the second gap, and it is widest in the regions. Six touring routes were rated Ready,
Developing or Gap on access, accommodation, safety and bookability.347 Route 62 has the lowest online
bookability of the six, and the West Coast route rates Gap on digital distribution and payments. 348
CapeNature takes about 70% of its reservations through its call centre rather than its online platform. 349
Even in the Metro, about 40% or more of smaller operators lack online booking. 350

The regions need beds and a booking route before marketing, with quality visible on booking platforms, as
in Valencia (section 2.1).

No committed money closes the regional gaps
Committed capital closes the air gaps, not the regional ones. The Metro's pipeline exceeds R30bn, led by
ACSA's R11.3bn airport overhaul, the R20bn V&A Granger Bay expansion and the R3.3bn MyCiTi Phase
2. Yet each region carries its own unfunded gap.351 The Cape Metro, rated 4.5, still lacks last-mile transit
to Chapman's Peak, Constantia and Cape Point, as well as small-operator online booking and township
product. The Cape Winelands, rated 3.4, has Cape Winelands Airport and a wider precinct of R16bn to
R20bn. It still has no transit inside the wine triangle, little non-wine product and no pricing for domestic
visitors. The Garden Route, rated 3.4, has the George terminal and a R9.4m landing-system upgrade, but
no international carrier, a winter trough and no transport between towns.

The Overberg, rated 2.7, has the R81.7m Agulhas lighthouse precinct and the R17m Malgas road. It lacks
accommodation towards De Hoop, bookability east of Hermanus and year-round product. The West
Coast, rated 2.5, has the completed R693m Malmesbury bypass and the Saldanha port upgrade. It lacks
accommodation depth and digital payments and has no airport. The Central Karoo and Route 62, rated
1.9, have the R62 reconstruction under way and rural broadband to 2026. They lack accommodation
depth and emergency support and have no airport.

The pipeline adds air, port and road capacity, but none of it buys the transit, beds, booking and product
the regions lack. Section 2.4 asks who will fund them.

Pillar 2 must therefore win seats into the gateway first, then build the corridors, beds and booking that
decide where growth lands.

      What this means for the strategy
      Rewords FA07, Win direct routes and add seats on full routes, and supplies its binding
      constraint. Eleven routes above 80%, fares likely to rise before seats and a runway that binds until
      about 2032; the base case passes the model's seat line by 2030.
      Supports FA07, FA08 and FA15. Airport capital is committed; cruise needs product, not berths;
      regional gaps have no committed capital.
      Validates FA09, Accelerate tourism-enabling internal mobility. Accessibility decays from 4.7 to
      1.0 and public transport is absent beyond the Metro and the Garden Route. Stakeholders name last-
      mile access as a barrier, which supports it.
      Validates FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA11, Align visitor
      experience in-destination. Strong assets sit in areas with little tourism use, and mid-market beds
      are missing. Stakeholders' account of a narrow product set supports FA10.
      Validates FA12, Balance impact on nature-based attractions. Flagships are full at peak while
      alternatives are underused, as stakeholders also report.
      Supports FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Bookability is weakest in the
      regions, and stakeholders report product that cannot be booked online.
      Supports FA02, rewords FA04 and validates FA05 and FA06 from the air side. Winter seats fall
      to 6.4% of the year in June. No seat flies direct to Asia today, so conversion runs through the hubs.
      The mature long-haul routes are the fullest, and routes to five regional markets run above 80% and
      are named for more capacity.




2.4 The enabling environment

The enabling system is thinnest where the strategy needs it most. The decisive levers sit
nationally, local tourism bodies' funding has been disrupted and the province has no
approved way to count its tourism jobs. In the communities, organisation rather than
distance keeps visitor spend out.

The binding levers sit with national government
The levers that most limit growth are held nationally. In an insight rated High, the Department of Economic
Development reports that "restrictive bilateral air agreements are limiting airlift growth", leaving the
province less competitive than peers.352 Chinese routes are "frequently an extension of government-to-
government relations".353 The runway that caps Cape Town International sits with ACSA, a national body
(sections 1.2 and 2.3).

Visas and national marketing follow the same pattern. Chinese and Gulf travellers face slow, document-
heavy visa processes.354 Four organisations raised visas, among them the Cableway for India and China
and the CTICC for African events.355 The Department of Economic Development also finds that SA
Tourism's institutional weakness constrains international marketing.356 The Western Cape holds none of
these levers (section 1.2), so it must move them by escalation backed with evidence. Chile's case points
the same way: lobby for air service liberalisation and simpler visas (section 2.1).



352
    Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p24; insights are the project team's statements, attributed to the organisation engaged.

    MIDAS Aviation Market Assessment, Mar 2026, "The Chinese Market Opportunity".

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                       DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Funding has thinned and mandates blur
The bodies that manage and sell the destination are weaker than a decade ago. The Department of
Economic Development rates Western Cape and Cape Town tourism funding "materially below peer
destinations" for destination management and marketing. It finds that tourism institutional capacity "has
eroded over the past decade at both provincial and municipal level", and both findings are rated High. 357

National Treasury's MFMA Circular No. 131 of 31 March 2025 has since disrupted established municipal
funding for local tourism organisations (LTOs), a finding also rated High. Most of them "are not
operationally sustainable without municipal funding support".358 Mandates also blur at district level. The
West Coast District Municipality reports "a mandate disconnect" between the district, local municipalities
and LTOs that leaves tourism development without a clear owner.359

Fiscal pressure is turning attention to a tourism levy and blended finance.360 The committed capital in
section 2.3 builds airports, ports, roads, transit and precincts, but none of it funds the bodies that manage
and sell the destination. The strategy cannot rest on LTOs until their funding and mandates are settled.

Climate losses already fall on the flagships
Climate risk is now an operating cost at Robben Island and in CapeNature's reserves. Robben Island
Museum loses about R16 million a year to weather-cancelled trips, a High-rated insight.361 CapeNature
reports climate-related flooding, fire and heat closing sites.362 Sustainable destination management is
therefore risk management for the province's flagship product today, and should be funded as such, not as
a later add-on.

Collaboration platforms exist; data does not flow
Collaboration and data sharing are the barriers stakeholders raise most often. Fifteen of the 16
organisations raised industry and government collaboration, and fifteen raised policy and data sharing.363

Figure 2.7. Themes raised by stakeholder organisations
Source: Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p20 to p46: number of the 16 organisations with at least one insight under each hypothesis-
tree element, counted from the appendix tables. The report prints no tally.

Data sharing also draws 30 insights and 16 High ratings, the most of any theme.364 Stakeholders describe
the data architecture as "too fragmented, lagged and proxy-based to provide a reliable view of visitor
volumes, spend, spread or economic impact at provincial level".365

Coordination is hard everywhere, as the comparators show (section 2.1). Yet the platforms for
collaboration already exist here. FEDHASA points to JAMS, through which tourism associations
coordinate with Wesgro, the Western Cape Government and the City of Cape Town.366 What is missing is
an agreed way to share data across those bodies, not another forum.

The province has no approved jobs count
The province cannot yet measure tourism's jobs or its contribution to GDP. The national Department of
Tourism reports that "there is no approved official methodology for provincial tourism GDP and jobs
estimates", rated High.367 Stats SA cites 749 vacancies behind delays to the tourism satellite account and
infers provincial flows partly from ports-of-entry data.368

The communities show the same gap at small scale. No community profile counts tourism employment
inside its boundary. The figures are node-side instead, such as about 4,700 formal Town Centre jobs for
82,393 employed Mitchells Plain residents.369 Need rests on fifteen-year-old Census data.370 CapeNature,
the West Coast District and the Overberg raise skills gaps, but no labour analysis sits behind them. 371

For the Western Cape, a jobs baseline must come before a jobs target. The ambition of 300,000 tourism-
linked jobs (section 1.1) has no baseline yet. The yield-based regional metrics that New Zealand
recommends (section 2.1) also need a reliable view of spend and spread, which today's data cannot give.

Communities hold people, not spend
Foreign visitors spent R25.9bn in the province in FY2025, but little of it reaches its townships and rural
settlements.372 The 20 shortlisted communities come from a long-list of every Census sub-place cluster of
1,500 people or more with official unemployment of 25% or more. Named mission, fishing and farm
settlements are added.373 That gives 107 communities holding 2.81 million people in 2011, 48% of the
province.374 Five weighted criteria (need, tourism potential, proximity, readiness and access) produce the
shortlist of 20, five in the Cape Metro and three in each district.375

The 20 hold 18.4% of the province's residents and 15.4% of employed residents (Census 2011), yet
receive 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend.376 This comes to about R4m to R10m a year on the
documented floor, against R259m a year if they captured 1% of FY2025 foreign direct spend. 377

Inclusion here means activity inside the community. A tour, a meal, a bed or a market counts; a resident
commuting to a hotel job in town does not.378 Inclusion therefore turns on where visitor spend lands, not on
how many visitors come.

Organisation, not distance, keeps spend out
Distance is not the barrier. Of the 107 long-listed communities, 93 lie within 45 minutes of one of 35
tourism nodes or three corridors.379 Langa is 12 minutes from Cape Town International and Zwelihle seven
from Hermanus.380 Distance is the binding constraint in none of the 20.381 Yet proximity and access carried
35% of the shortlist score, so the result is partly built into the selection (Appendix A5, "Community shortlist
criteria").

Organisation is the binding constraint in 11 of the 20. Ten have no identified active tourism body, and nine
show no benefit visible to residents.382

Figure 2.8. Binding constraints in 20 communities

Product often exists without a seller. Langa's walk sells on global platforms at ratings above 4.8, yet city
firms sell most of the 23 Langa walks on Viator.383 Zwelihle's four resident guides sell by phone with no
shared booking channel.384 The Mitchells Plain festival drew 27,800 visitors in November 2025 with no
local operator or LTO behind it.385 The communities cannot yet be found or booked on the channels
visitors use. What they lack is a local seller, not a shorter journey.

Safety is the second gate
After a seller, safety is the next gate a visit must pass. Nine of the 20 communities record a safety-
perception gap and five record extortion.386 Seven stakeholder organisations raised safety, a barrier that
runs across transport, township tourism and international selling.387 Tourism cannot fix this alone: "tourism
activity alone cannot resolve crime or extortion".388 Safety is therefore a launch requirement in its own
right, not an outcome of the pilot. No community pilot should open until it is met.

Each community type needs a different first move
The largest group of long-listed communities sits near a node and has something worth visiting, and each
type needs its own first move.389 Near a node with assets underused, 49 long-listed and 11 shortlisted
communities need sellers organised and a slot in the node's itineraries, procurement and events. Near a
node with nothing inside, 43 long-listed and 6 shortlisted communities need a seller first, then one anchor
asset under a covenant, then integration.

Remote communities with underused assets, 7 long-listed and 2 shortlisted, need access opened and the
asset placed on a route product the LTO already sells. Remote communities with nothing inside, 8 long-

A seller with a stake comes first
The evidence points to a model built around a seller. It joins a functioning selling capability to demand the
node already has, behind launch gates for safety, access and a viable operating model.390 A pilot in two or
three of six profiled communities would test it. It would scale only once bookings, local spend, delivery and
safety meet thresholds agreed before launch.391

The first output is deliberately small: "one live booking route, published operating dates, a named
customer contact and a simple record of bookings and local revenue".392 Most first moves carry no
dedicated budget line.393 Chapter 4 sets out roles and funding. The order the cases support (section 2.1)
therefore becomes the order of delivery: a seller first, then a safe visit, then product and exposure.

Every gap in this part is a question of who decides, who pays, who measures and who sells. Pillar 3 must
settle those four questions, because Pillars 1 and 2 cannot deliver without the answers.

      What this means for the strategy
      Refines FA13, Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion. The binding constraint
      is a seller with a stake, then a safe way in, then demand pulled from the adjacent node.
      Rewords FA14, Manage climate risk and sustainable tourism, and supports FA15, Crowd in
      capital with targeted investment strategies. Unfunded local tourism bodies and climate losses at
      flagship sites.
      Supports FA10, Support diversified route and product development, and FA15. Assets follow
      sellers and route products, not the reverse.
      Rewords FA16, Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers, and supports FA19, Lead
      removal of infrastructure, visa and safety deficits. Air rights, visas and SA Tourism sit nationally,
      Chinese routes follow government-to-government agreements and airport capacity sits with ACSA;
      mandates blur at district level.
      Supplies the visa barriers for FA19 and supports it on safety. Chinese and Gulf travellers face
      slow, document-heavy visa processes. Safety and extortion gate activation in up to nine communities.
      Validates FA17, Strengthen industry and government collaboration, and FA18, Expedite
      tourism-enabling policy and data sharing. Each raised by 15 of 16 organisations, with existing
      platforms such as JAMS to build on.
      Supplies the case for FA20, Develop workforce and service skills, as a question; supports
      FA20 and FA18 on measurement. Skills gaps raised by CapeNature, the West Coast and the
      Overberg have no labour analysis behind them.394 No count of tourism jobs exists inside the
      communities, and need rests on Census 2011.
      Supports FA03, Make the whole province findable and bookable. Community product sells by
      phone or through city operators.

3.1 Pillar 1: Reshape demand
Latent interest is not what holds Pillar 1 back; turning it into bookings is. The province is
known in its mature markets, under-sold in its growth markets and hard to book outside its
core.

The December tree gave the pillar one objective: break through the market, seasonality and spread
ceilings by reshaping the brand toward new traveller segments.395 The evidence confirmed all three
ceilings.

      •   Markets. Five markets supplied 63% of overseas arrivals in 2025, and the UK, Germany and the
          USA supplied 52% between them.396
      •   Seasons. June held 6.4% of the year's international seats at Cape Town International in 2025,
          against 10.8% in December.397
      •   Spread. Most rural properties have no OTA presence and are invisible to the market.398
What changed between December and October is the diagnosis. The Market Assessment counts about
97 million high-value travellers in 34 markets with an interest in South Africa, of whom about 1.4 million
convert into arrivals.399 The Diagnostic had already named the constraint as conversion and access, not
only awareness.400 Three of the six entries were reworded toward conversion: green-season seats,
bookable product and hub traffic already in the air.

Pillar 1 carries most of the base case. Between 2025 and 2035 the base case adds 676,949 arrivals. 401
The four hub markets supply 206,584, the mature markets 178,780, the acceleration markets 175,818,
regional Africa 110,298 and the strategic group 5,469. None of it lands without seats, so the air access
entry in Pillar 2 runs beside this pillar from the first phase.

Pillar 1 holds six focus areas: three carried through as Validated and three were reworded toward
conversion. The entries follow, and the executive summary lists them with their initiatives. Dispositions are
as recorded at the 14 October 2026 review [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck page
reference to be confirmed].

3.1.1 Traveller, brand and proposition
The first theme is about whom the Western Cape sells to, when and how. It holds three focus areas: the
segments, the green season and the bookability of the province. The theme name carries through from
December unchanged.

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 1


Respond to growth segment traveller preferences
Four traveller segments, each concentrated in named home markets, give Wesgro a sharper
target than country alone, and the province scores 3.7 to 4.6 out of 5 on its ability to win
them.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Respond to Growth Segment Traveller Preferences.402 The wording is unchanged in
every reprint of the tree.

October 2026: Validated. Four segments were confirmed against markets of high incidence. The Skift
segmentation of March 2026 drove the disposition, and the Market Assessment carried it into the demand
work.

Evidence
The segmentation rests on a May 2025 survey of more than 25,000 outbound travellers in 34 markets,
applied to 17 priority markets.403

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 2


Create green-season demand with MICE and events
The green season is a seat problem and an events problem at once. June holds 6.4% of the
year's international seats, and business events are the lever best placed to fill the trough,
though international meetings now peak from September to November.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Develop the Seasonality and Spread Proposition,410 reprinted from March 2026 as
Develop seasonality, spread and Mega events proposition.411

October 2026: Reworded. June holds 6.4% of seats, and ICCA meetings cluster from May to November.
Spread moved to focus areas 3 and 10, and business events replaced mega events as the lever. MIDAS's
seasonality analysis and the Demand-Side MICE landscape drove the change.

Evidence
Seats and visitors both fall away from April to September. Business events do not.

THEME 1.1 · TRAVELLER, BRAND AND PROPOSITION · FOCUS AREA 3


Make the whole province findable and bookable
Outside the Cape Peninsula, the Winelands and the Garden Route, most product cannot be
booked where travellers book, so interest the province has already earned has nowhere to
land.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Facilitate Adoption Along the Path to Purchase.419

October 2026: Reworded. Community product is unbookable, CapeNature takes about 70% of its
reservations by phone and rural stock has OTA gaps. The focus moved from source-market platforms to
the province's own product being invisible and unbookable. The Inclusion Plan, the stakeholder interviews
and the Infrastructure Report drove the change.

Recommendation
Wesgro should treat bookability as infrastructure: count it, close it district by district and then place it on
the platforms each market uses. The Inclusion Plan sets a community's first deliverable as one live
booking route, published operating dates, a named customer contact and a simple record of bookings and
local revenue.421 Growth markets book on their own platforms: Trip.com and Fliggy in China, MakeMyTrip
in India and Almosafer in the Gulf.422

3.1.2 Source market growth and diversification
The second theme is about which markets grow the base. It holds one focus area per market group: India
and China, which reach the province through hubs; the mature long-haul markets; and regional Africa. The
theme name carries through from December unchanged.

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 4


Convert India and China hub traffic
About 101,000 Indian and Chinese travellers a year already reach Cape Town, mainly
through Gulf and African hubs, and with no direct Indian route likely before 2030, converting
that flow is the lever available now.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Pivot to Growth Markets (China, India, ME, ASEAN).425

October 2026: Reworded, with the scope narrowed. About 101,000 travellers a year already fly in via
hubs. The Middle East and ASEAN left the title, and the lever narrowed from pivoting to converting traffic
already in the air. The Demand-Side route-to-market work and MIDAS drove the change. The Middle East
stays in the growth model's acceleration group.

Evidence

Table 3.4. Evidence for focus area 4

      India and China reach Cape Town only through hubs, and the direct route that would change that is at
      least five years away.

Wesgro should run India and China as conversion markets through the hubs and as relationship markets

THEME 1.2 · SOURCE MARKET GROWTH AND DIVERSIFICATION · FOCUS AREA 5


Increase yield in mature markets
The mature markets already pay the most and fill the fullest routes, so growth there comes
from more value per visitor and more seats on existing routes, not from more marketing.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Increase Yield in Mature Markets (UK, Europe, N. America).431

October 2026: Validated, without fare or yield data. Full routes. The October title drops the list of
markets. MIDAS drove the disposition: the mature long-haul routes run above 80% load, and carriers on
full routes raise fares before they add seats.

Wesgro should protect share and raise value per visitor in the mature markets, and work the full routes for
frequency and gauge before it chases new ones. The Demand-Side Analysis frames the job as protecting
share, increasing yield and driving regional dispersal while new growth markets are developed.432 MIDAS
sees tactical room for added frequency on some routes and larger aircraft from some airlines. 433

3.2 Pillar 2: Transform supply

Supply sets the ceiling on the growth case. The airport caps arrivals before its relief arrives,
and outside the Metro the product exists but is hard to reach, book or stay in.

The December tree asked the pillar to remove mobility, infrastructure and product constraints so as to
expand the province's physical carrying capacity.443 The evidence confirmed the objective and located
each constraint.

      •   The gateway. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million inbound seats,
          and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point;444 the runway binds at peak hours. The
          international terminal upgrade at Cape Town International is due about 2032 and the runway about
          2033.445
      •   The regions. Accessibility scores fall from 4.7 in the Cape Metro to 1.0 in the Central Karoo, and
          one touring route of six is rated Ready.446
      •   The product. The constraint is less about creating new attractions and more about turning existing
          assets into complete, accessible visitor experiences.447
Two structural changes happened in this pillar. The December entry on air access and cruise split in two,
because MIDAS gave air access its own evidence base. The Infrastructure Report found cruise to be a
product and marketing lever, with Cape Town's berth at about 40% of its season capacity.448 The entry on
infrastructure, visa and safety deficits moved to Pillar 3 because its levers sit nationally (section 3.3.2).

The Infrastructure Report reads transport as a connectivity and dispersal challenge, not a province-wide
capacity crisis, with Cape Town International the key gateway risk.449 Pillar 2 follows that reading: one
capacity entry at the gateway and five entries about reach, product and experience.

Pillar 2 holds six focus areas: the air entry was reworded, cruise is new and the four others carried through
as Validated. The entries follow, and the executive summary lists them with their initiatives. Dispositions
are as recorded at the 14 October 2026 review [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck
page reference to be confirmed].

3.2.1 Accessibility, connectivity and infrastructure
The first supply theme covers how visitors reach the province and move through it. It holds three focus
areas: air access, cruise and internal mobility. The December entry on visa and safety deficits left this
theme for section 3.3.2.

THEME 2.1 · ACCESSIBILITY, CONNECTIVITY AND INFRASTRUCTURE · FOCUS AREA 7


Win direct routes and add seats on full routes
Eleven of 27 international routes run above 80% load and carriers raise fares before seats,
so the province must win frequency and larger aircraft now, ahead of runway relief in about
2033.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Close the Air Access and Cruise Capacity Gaps.450 The air half of the entry became this
focus area and the cruise half became focus area 8.

October 2026: Reworded. The reason is full routes and fares before seats: eleven routes run above 80%
load and sixteen at 75% or more.451 MIDAS and the Infrastructure Report's air-access section drove the
change.

Recommendation
Wesgro should split the air agenda by horizon. Until about 2032 the job is to fill and enlarge what already

capacity arrives. The Demand-Side Analysis lists the moves: added frequency from Emirates, Qatar

incentives through Cape Town Air Access.454

Benchmark precedent
Chile treated its gateway airport as economic infrastructure: a private concession funded a ~US$900
million expansion at Santiago, and international seats grew ~40% to 50% between 2013 and 2019 under
open-skies agreements.455 For the Western Cape the lesson splits by tier. The province can scale its
existing route fund; open skies and private airport finance are national levers, so this report carries them in
the escalation agenda (focus area 19). Chile's 2024 surge also came mostly overland: land borders
carried 55.6% of arrivals and Argentina about 40%.456

corridor through Muizenberg, Kalk Bay, Boulders and Simon's Town.466 On the West Coast, !Khwa ttu
says a shared shuttle needs demand aggregation and booking integration to work.467

3.2.2 Product and destination capacity
The second supply theme covers what visitors find once they arrive: product beyond the icons, the quality
of the stay and the limits of the flagship sites. All three December entries carried through as Validated.

Support diversified route and product development
The province does not lack attractions; it lacks finished experiences around them, with
assets outside the Metro left without guides, interpretation or bookable product and one
touring route of six rated Ready.

theme. Cold zones is the report's own term; idle assets paraphrases its finding that assets are inactivated
or underused.

THEME 2.2 · PRODUCT AND DESTINATION CAPACITY · FOCUS AREA 12


Balance impact on nature-based attractions
Boulders is near carrying capacity and Cableway queues reach three hours, so growth at
the flagship nature sites must come from managing flows and steering visitors to under-
marketed reserves.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Balance Impact on Nature-Based Attractions.482

October 2026: Validated. Flagship limits. The stakeholder evidence on Boulders and Table Mountain and
the Infrastructure Report's record of flagship congestion drove the disposition. The operators and the
province state the limits; no deliverable prints a visitor cap or carrying-capacity figure for any flagship site.

No deliverable prints annual visitors or permitted capacity for Table Mountain, Boulders, Robben Island,

Robben Island Museum and SANBI]. The only printed daily cap in hand is the City's permit system at the
Witzands dunes near Atlantis, which is 80% pre-booked online.483

Recommendation
Wesgro should stop marketing volume to the flagship sites and use them as the hook that sends visitors
elsewhere. The Department of Economic Development wants further growth directed to under-visited
parts of the province rather than constrained flagships.484 The Peninsula route's own opportunities are to
disperse visitors beyond the highest-volume attractions and to expand shoulder-season experiences.485
The site operators hold the flow tools, from digital flow management to tiered pricing.486

3.3 Pillar 3: Build enabling capability

Pillar 3 decides whether the other two can be delivered and shared: who benefits, who pays,
who decides and who measures.

The December tree asked the pillar to build an inclusive, well-governed tourism system that supports
sustainable growth, returns and development.489 The evidence found each part of that system thin.

      •   Inclusion. Twenty township and rural communities hold 18.4% of the province's residents but
          receive a traced 0.02% to 0.04% of foreign direct spend.490
      •   Investment. The capital pipeline funds airports, ports, roads and rail, and none of it reaches tourism
          product, bookability or community assets.491
      •   Governance. Visas and air service agreements sit with national government, and mobility
          mandates are split across three tiers.492
      •   Measurement. No approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP and jobs.493
This pillar changed most between December and October, and now holds eight focus areas. The inclusion
entry was refined and held at concept level. Sustainability was reworded to climate risk and local
destination funding, and institutional review to three-tier alignment. The deficits entry moved in from Pillar
2, and workforce skills was added and flagged as untested.

The evidence here is mostly qualitative. It rests on attributed stakeholder statements, the Diagnostic's
one-slide tier table, the Inclusion Plan and the Infrastructure Report's gap tables, so this pillar carries more
open items than the other two.

Pillar 3 holds eight focus areas, the widest spread of dispositions in the tree. The entries follow, and the
executive summary lists them with their initiatives. Dispositions are as recorded at the 14 October 2026
review [Summary of Findings, 14 Oct 2026, pp15 to 16: deck page reference to be confirmed].

3.3.1 Investment, inclusion and sustainability
The first enabling theme covers who benefits from growth, how it is paid for and how it lasts. It holds three
focus areas: community inclusion, climate risk and investment. The inclusion entry stays at concept level,
as agreed with Wesgro on 24 September 2026; roles and funding sit in Chapter 4.

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 13


Embed local destination bodies for community inclusion
Twenty communities hold 18.4% of the province's residents but receive only a small
documented share of foreign direct spend, and in eleven the binding constraint is
organisation, not distance or product.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Drive Community and SME Inclusion.494

October 2026: Refined. A seller with a stake, 20 communities and R259m at 1%. The SME and
Community Inclusion Plan drove the refinement. As agreed with Wesgro on 24 September 2026, the entry
stays at concept level, and roles and funding are set in Chapter 4.

Evidence

Table 3.13. Evidence for focus area 13

      The shortlisted communities sit close to visitors but earn almost nothing from them, and most lack a body
      that can sell.

The plan does not describe how the spend floor was traced. The multiple of today's floor is computed.

[Open item: the Inclusion Plan to set out how the R4m to R10m floor was traced.]

The plan's five cases set the order of action: a seller with a stake, a safe and workable visit, product that
keeps spend in the community, then exposure.495 Kliptown in Soweto shows the cost of the reverse order.
Walter Sisulu Square cost R160 million to R180 million, out of the R436 million first allocated to Kliptown
(section 2.1), before an operator was in place, and drew about 4,000 museum visitors a year by 2017
against a business case of almost 200,000 international visitors.496

Recommendation
Wesgro should connect community product to the demand it already moves, and leave the building of
selling bodies to municipalities, LTOs and the communities themselves. The plan's model has three parts:
a selling capability that works, a link to demand that already exists and gates that must be passed before
activation and investment.497 Two or three pilot communities test it, and it scales only after agreed
thresholds are met.498 The plan's sequence runs from validating the offer and opening a booking route,
through a safety plan and a fixed-date test, to investment after demand and scale through channels. 499

Benchmark precedent
Kenya writes community participation into its tourism governance and investment system through
community conservancies, with benefits across rural communities among the listed outcomes. 500 The
plan's own cases carry the figures: Medellín's Comuna 13 drew 1.98 million visitors in 2025 around
resident-run product, while Kliptown shows what capital before an operator buys.501

Climate is already costing the province sites, routes and revenue, while the local tourism
bodies that would manage the response depend on municipal funding that MFMA Circular
No. 131 has disrupted.

December 2025. Ensure Sustainable Destination Management.502

October 2026: Reworded. LTOs are unfunded and the Meiringspoort flood cut a route. Destination-

evidence on LTO funding and climate losses drove the change, with the Inclusion Plan's De Rust profile
for the flood.

THEME 3.1 · INVESTMENT, INCLUSION AND SUSTAINABILITY · FOCUS AREA 15


Crowd in capital with targeted investment strategies
The province has a capital pipeline for airports, ports, roads and rail, but none of it reaches
tourism product, bookability, accommodation depth or community assets, which is where the
growth case needs investment.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Crowd-In Capital with Targeted Investment Strategies.507

October 2026: Validated, on inference. No pipeline. The Infrastructure Report's pipeline and gap-closure
tables drove the disposition, with the stakeholder view that funding sits below peers and the Chilean and
New Zealand benchmarks. The reason is an inference: the report lists a large capital pipeline, and its gap
columns show that none of it reaches tourism product.

3.3.2 Governance and leadership
The second enabling theme covers who decides and how the plan is run. It holds five focus areas, the
most of any theme. They are three-tier alignment, industry collaboration, data and policy, the national
deficits moved in from Pillar 2 and workforce skills, which was added in October and flagged as untested.

THEME 3.2 · GOVERNANCE AND LEADERSHIP · FOCUS AREA 16


Align institutional stakeholders across all three tiers
Air service agreements and visas sit with national government, mandates are split across
three tiers and the national marketing body is under strain, so the province needs one
shared agenda with clear decision rights.

Hypothesis and disposition
December 2025. Review Institutional Arrangements (Wesgro, DEDAT, CTT),513 reprinted from March
2026 as Review Institutional Arrangements (local, provincial and national).

October 2026: Reworded. Air rights and visas sit nationally. The review of three named bodies widened
to alignment across national, provincial and local government. The stakeholder evidence on bilateral
agreements and split mandates, the Diagnostic's tier table and MIDAS's account of route designation
drove the change.

Evidence

3.4 The integrated case

The three pillars make one case. Demand work fills seats only if the seats exist, supply work
pays only if the product can be booked, and both need the enabling base to last.

The growth model sets out three paths from the 2025 base of 1,498,829 arrivals: 1,822,634 in 2035 on the
conservative path, 2,175,778 on the base path and 2,516,282 on the accelerated path.546 The deck is
explicit that the paths differ by intervention, not by demand potential.547 Each step of the growth case
therefore rests on a named set of interventions, and each set draws on focus areas in all three pillars.

The conservative path, 1,822,634 arrivals in 2035, holds existing air access with no added frequencies or
routes and continues trade and marketing, with growth in the mature markets and current corridors. Focus
areas 5 and 7 hold that base. The base path adds 353,144 arrivals to reach 2,175,778. It assumes
Emirates, Qatar, Ethiopian and Turkish Airlines add frequency and capacity and existing Wesgro
programmes expand, while India, China and regional Africa grow through hub connections. Focus areas 1
to 7, 9 and 10 deliver it. The accelerated path adds a further 340,504 to reach 2,516,282. It needs direct
India and China routes secured and dedicated activation in India, China, the USA and the GCC, with
India, China and the Middle East growing ahead of their source markets. Focus areas 4, 7, 16 and 19
deliver it on top of the base.548

The gateway binds first, at peak. The growth model draws current air access as a line at 1.8 million
inbound seats, and the base case crosses it at the 2030 year point.549 Relief at Cape Town International
arrives about 2032 for the international terminal and about 2033 for the runway.550 ACSA now schedules
the realigned runway for December 2029, subject to contract award. This plan keeps about 2033 as its
planning date and treats the earlier date as upside.551 Between those dates, growth has to come from
larger aircraft and added frequency on the full routes, from hub-carrier capacity and, for domestic
connections, from George, whose terminal doubles by 2028 or 2029 (focus areas 5, 6 and 7).

The deck draws arrivals and seats against one axis and prints no seat table behind the 1.8 million line. The
crossing year is read from the chart: after the 2028 point (1,673,855) and by the 2030 point (1,802,929).

If the supply moves slip, the case stops at the seat line. The deck states that the projected growth
cannot be realised without additional capacity or frequency,552 and that without confirmed routes the
accelerated case reverts to base.553 On the full routes, demand work cannot make up the gap, because
marketing raises fares rather than seats;554 on the hub routes, which have seats to spare, it can.

Below the gateway, the pillars depend on each other. Green-season demand needs seats in the
months carriers now cut (focus areas 2 and 7). Dispersal needs product that can be booked and reached
(focus areas 3, 9 and 10). Inclusion needs a seller and a safe visit before exposure (focus areas 13 and
19). Every target needs data that today sits with SA Tourism, ACSA and the booking platforms (focus area
18).

Air access and bookability move first, because every other lever runs through them. The first phase
works the seats and the booking path before the base case crosses the model's seat line, and the direct
routes wait for runway relief. Chapter 4 turns the sequence into eighteen initiatives with owners, phases
and decision gates.

Activate, 2027 to 2028, comes before the base case crosses the model's seat line around 2030. Seats
are held and added on the full routes (focus areas 5, 6 and 7), the green-season calendar is set (2) and
the bookability census and onboarding run (3). Two or three inclusion pilots start (13), Steerco, the data
protocol and the escalation agenda are set up (16, 18 and 19) and the labour analysis is commissioned
(20).

Expand, 2029 to 2031, brings hub conversion at scale (4), corridor product and mobility (9 and 10), the
quality charter (11) and flagship flow management (12), along with cruise product (8) and the investment
pipeline at financial close (15). The 2030 mid-term review tests the seat line, and terminal relief arrives
from about 2032.

Sustain, 2032 to 2035, takes direct India and China routes to carriers as runway capacity arrives (4 and
7), scales inclusion once its thresholds are met (13) and embeds climate risk and LTO funding (14).
Runway relief arrives about 2033, and the accelerated case depends on the direct routes. 555

Chapter 4


Execution plan
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                         DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




4.1 From strategy to initiatives
Twenty focus areas become eighteen initiatives, one per focus area except where one
owner delivers two.

The plan follows one chain from vision to measure. Each level answers a different question. The pillar
says what must change and the focus area says where; the initiative says what Wesgro and its partners
do, and the indicator says how Steerco will know it has happened (section 5.1).

Seven levels run from the vision to the measures, and each has a fixed count in this plan. One vision says
where the province is going: grow international arrivals from 1.5m in 2025 toward 2.5m by 2035. Three
pillars say what must change, such as Reshape demand. Six themes say in which part of the system,
such as source market growth and diversification. Twenty focus areas say where the evidence says to act,
such as Convert India and China hub traffic. Eighteen initiatives say what Wesgro and its partners do,
such as I4 India and China hub conversion. The 112 actions say who does what, by tier, such as
activation inside Xiaohongshu, Douyin and MakeMyTrip. The indicators, 7 at impact, 25 at outcome and
36 at output level, say how Steerco knows, such as T2-06 India and China hub arrivals. The counts come
from Chapter 3, the action tables and the traceability table in Appendix A1.

Two merges take twenty focus areas to eighteen initiatives. Segment-led marketing and mature-market
yield share one owner and one set of channels, so focus areas 1 and 5 form initiative I1. Institutional
alignment and industry collaboration share one forum, so focus areas 16 and 17 form I15. Every other
focus area has its own initiative, numbered I1 to I18 in one sequence so that the M&E toolkit can track
them on one list.

Leads are proposed, not agreed. They follow the Wesgro units named in the Inception Report's
stakeholder map and the actions in Chapter 3, and Steerco confirms them at its first meeting [Steerco to
confirm initiative leads and partners]. Six initiatives have a partner as the accountable lead, because the
main lever sits outside Wesgro's mandate: corridor mobility, quality standards, flagship sites, community
pilots, LTO funding and skills.

4.2 Sequencing and phases

Three phases run to 2035, and the first is weighted to the moves that change the 2030
constraint: seats, bookability, green-season events and the inclusion pilots.

The phases follow calendar years: Activate from 2027 to 2028, Expand from 2029 to 2031 and Sustain
from 2032 to 2035. Each phase ends at a decision gate tied to the Chapter 5 indicators (section 4.7). The
first gate, at the end of 2028, falls in Wesgro's FY2028/29, two years before the base case crosses the
model's seat line around 2030.556




Figure 4.1. Initiative timeline, 2027 to 2035
Source: Letsema, initiative portfolio (sections 4.1 and 4.3). Design means scoping, data, agreements and pilots; implement means delivery at
scale.


Phase 1: Activate, 2027 to 2028
Phase 1 works inside Wesgro's mandate and budget and on the levers that move before the airport can. It
holds and adds seats on the full routes and builds the air access fund (I6). It publishes the green-season
calendar (I2), counts and starts to close the bookability gap (I3) and starts two or three inclusion pilots
(I12). Steerco, the data-sharing protocol and the escalation agenda start in the first quarter (I15, I16, I17),
because every other initiative reports through them.

The marketing initiatives (I1, I4, I5) and cruise (I7) spend 2027 on design: segment targets, hub-carrier
agreements, the Nigeria and Kenya plans and excursion products. Seven initiatives use both years for
design (I8 to I11, I13, I14, I18). Their first task is a baseline or a case that no deliverable holds: corridor
556
      Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p21.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                     116
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                       DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



mobility, product, quality, flagship capacity, climate risk and LTO funding, the investment pipeline and the
labour analysis. The gate at the end of 2028 asks three questions. Have seats on the full routes held?
Does the bookability baseline exist and is it moving? Has any pilot met its thresholds?

Phase 2: Expand, 2029 to 2031
Phase 2 scales what Phase 1 proved and meets the model's seat line. Hub conversion, regional Africa
and segment marketing run at scale. Corridor mobility and product move from design to delivery, on the
Garden Route, the Winelands and the Overberg first. The quality charter is adopted and phased by
district, and the investment pipeline reaches financial close on its first projects. The mid-term review in
2030 tests arrivals against the base path when the base case crosses the model's seat line, and checks
that the runway is on its published dates (section 5.6).

Phase 3: Sustain, 2032 to 2035
Phase 3 runs as relief arrives at Cape Town International: the international terminal about 2032 and the
runway about 2033.557 The direct India and China route cases built since 2027 go to carriers as capacity
frees up, which is the step the accelerated case depends on. Inclusion pilots that met their thresholds
scale through channels, climate risk and LTO funding are embedded in municipal planning, and the
terminal evaluation in 2035 closes the plan (section 5.6).




557
      Infrastructure Report, Jul 2026, p103.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                       117
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                     DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




4.3 Initiative charters

Each initiative has a one-page charter that a lead can run from and Steerco can hold to
account.

Every charter uses the same fields. It states the objective, the focus areas served, the proposed lead and
partners and the tier of government. It then sets the actions in each phase, marked Design or Implement,
and closes with the dependencies, the funding route, the risks and the indicators it moves. Actions come
from the Chapter 3 action tables. Leads are proposals for Steerco to confirm, and rand bands are set in
section 4.6.




Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                    118
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                           DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



I1 Segment-led activation and yield in mature markets
 Field                     Charter
 Objective                 Raise value per visitor from the mature markets and target every priority market by segment, not
                           by country alone.
 Focus areas               1, 5
 Proposed lead             Destination Marketing
 Partners                  Research; trade and OTA partners; SA Tourism
 Tier of government        Provincial (Wesgro); national alignment with SA Tourism
 Activate, 2027 to         Design (2027): segment targets inside each priority market; a shared segment frame with SA
 2028                      Tourism; destination-specific segment research. Implement (2028): move spend from broad
                           awareness to trade-led conversion; luxury-advisor and high-end operator distribution in the UK,
                           USA, Germany, France and Switzerland.
 Expand, 2029 to           Segment campaigns at scale; spend per visitor and length of stay added to Wesgro's own targets;
 2031                      segments built into brand tracking.
 Sustain, 2032 to          Segment research refreshed; budget shifted toward the segments and markets with the highest
 2035                      yield.
 Dependencies              I6 for seats on the full routes; I16 for spend-per-visitor data; I10 for product quality.
 Funding route             Main: Wesgro operating budget. Supporting: private capital. Rand band to be set (section 4.6).
 Risks                     Carriers on full routes raise fares rather than seats; SA Tourism's institutional weakness limits joint
                           campaigns (Stakeholder Insights Report, Mar 2026, p25).
 Indicators                T1-01, T1-02, T1-03; T2-01, T2-07; T3-01 segment targets in Wesgro's annual plan; T3-02 share
                           of market budget in trade-led conversion.

4.4 Roles and accountabilities

Wesgro is accountable for twelve initiatives and does part of the work in all eighteen; for the
other six, the accountable lead sits with another tier, and the plan names it.

The matrix below assigns each initiative to the four roles: responsible (R) for doing the work, accountable
(A) for the result, consulted (C) before decisions and informed (I) of progress. Where Wesgro is
accountable and does the work itself, the cell reads A/R. Assignments are proposals for Steerco to
confirm, built from the actions in Chapter 3.

Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                         DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



National government and the state-owned entities
Four levers sit outside the province and cannot be bought or marketed into place. Route rights and visas
sit with the national tier,558 the airport and port capital programmes with national SOEs559 and policing with
SAPS. The plan treats them as an escalation agenda (I17), carried by Steerco through DEDAT to the
national departments and SOEs. Each item is stated as a decision, an owner and a date the province is
asking for. Wesgro's own route work (I6) runs inside the existing agreements meanwhile.

Provincial government
DEDAT is a standing member of Steerco and the accountable lead where the lever is policy or funding
rather than promotion: quality standards (I10), LTO funding and climate risk (I13) and the labour analysis
(I18). The Department of Mobility leads the transport half of corridor mobility (I8). Wesgro's units lead the
demand, access, product and data initiatives and report through the programme office.

District and local government
Municipalities and LTOs hold the assets, the selling bodies and the visitor services that the regional and
inclusion initiatives depend on. They lead the community selling bodies in the pilots (I12), attach
covenants to public assets (I12, I14) and carry the route and product work in their districts (I3, I9). The City
of Cape Town and Cape Town Tourism are partners on events, mobility and data (I2, I8, I16) and sit on
Steerco.

The private sector and site operators
Airlines decide frequency and gauge, hotel investors decide the mid-scale tier and attraction operators
manage their own capacity. The plan gives them the evidence and the demand case. It asks for the
decisions it cannot make for them: seats on full routes (I6), investment in secondary nodes (I14) and
visitor-flow management at flagship sites, where the operators are accountable (I11).

4.6 Funding and investment

Most of the plan runs on budgets that already exist; the capital it needs sits with ACSA,
PRASA, Transnet and private investors, and none of it has been costed yet.

No deliverable costs any initiative. The Inception Report scoped an investment opportunity framework with
return scoring, but no separate output was delivered,560 and stakeholders describe Western Cape tourism
funding as materially below peer destinations without giving a figure.561 Thirteen initiatives draw mainly on
Wesgro's operating budget, and four need private capital as a main source; the initiatives table in section
4.1 gives each one's main and supporting funding routes. This section leaves the rand bands to be set
with Wesgro Finance and DEDAT [cost band per initiative and phase: Wesgro Finance and DEDAT to set
before Steerco signs off the plan].




Figure 4.2. Initiatives by main funding source
Source: Letsema, count of initiatives by main funding source from Table 4.1. An initiative can have more than one main source.

Crowding in capital. The rule for capital is the Inclusion Plan's: invest after demand is shown, and attach
a maintenance and marketing covenant to any public asset.562 Wesgro's investment promotion work builds
the tourism pipeline from the Infrastructure Report's gap-closure tables, where every district lists tourism
gaps with no committed capital (I14).563 The air access fund scales Cape Town Air Access's existing route
fund with public and private money (I6).

What the SLA asks and what is still to do. The SLA asks for return projections on infrastructure
priorities (clause 1.1.8.3) [clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B]. Those need
costed projects, and the only costed items in hand are the airport, rail and port programmes the province
does not fund. The return case is therefore an output of I14 in Phase 1, built project by project as the
pipeline forms.

4.7 Delivery governance

One Steerco, one programme office and four fixed decision points keep eighteen initiatives
and three tiers of government on one plan.




Figure 4.3. Delivery governance structure
Source: Letsema, proposed delivery governance.

Steerco. A Tourism Growth Steerco co-chaired by Wesgro's CEO and DEDAT's Head of Department,
with Cape Town Tourism as a standing member and the City of Cape Town, the district municipalities and
the industry bodies at the table. It is distinct from the Unlocking Tourism Steerco that oversees this study.
It receives the quarterly scorecard, clears cross-initiative issues, holds the escalation agenda and
recommends gate decisions to the Board. Stakeholders asked for this kind of alignment directly: mobility
responsibilities alone are split across three tiers of government.564

Programme office. A small office inside Wesgro runs the plan on a build, operate and transfer basis. It
builds the charters, the dashboard and the reporting calendar in Phase 1, runs them through Phase 2 and
hands them to the line units by the end of 2031. It owns the scorecard and the dashboard, and Wesgro
Research owns the indicator definitions and the data protocol (I16).

Cadence. Initiative leads report monthly to the programme office, Steerco meets quarterly and the Board
receives an annual outlook, which also anchors the Unlocking Tourism business outlook event. The plan
is refreshed each year against the indicators and the gate decisions.

Table 4.4. Decision gates and reviews

      Four decision points test the plan against its indicators, and each one decides what the next phase funds.

5.1 Results chain
Every initiative is measured at three levels: what it produces, what changes because of it
and what that adds to the 2035 case.

The framework is the written form of the M&E toolkit the SLA requires (clauses 1.1.4 and 1.1.10). It sets
indicators, baselines and targets for arrivals, spend, jobs, airlift, cruise and SMME inclusion, with the tools
to track them [clause wording to be checked against the signed Annexure B]. The Inception Report adds
geographic spread and asks for definitions, sources, frequency, owners and governance for each
indicator.565




Figure 5.1. Results chain for the plan
Source: Letsema, results chain for the plan.

The table below gives the chain for each initiative, pillar by pillar. Tier 3 output indicators come from the
initiative charters, Tier 2 outcome indicators from the focus areas and Tier 1 impact indicators from the
growth case.

Table 5.1. Results chain by initiative

      (I6 to I11) move seats, cruise volumes and the share of visitors and spend outside the Metro. Pillar 3
      initiatives (I12 to I18) move community spend and jobs, and supply the governance and data every other
      indicator relies on.

5.2 Indicator set

Seven impact indicators, twenty-five outcome indicators and thirty-six output indicators
measure the plan, each with a definition, a source, an owner and a frequency.

Tier 1 measures the 2035 case and is reported to the Board. Tier 2 measures the focus areas, one or two
each, and is reported to Steerco each quarter where the source allows. Tier 3 measures the initiatives'
own outputs and is reported by the leads to the programme office each month. The full register, with
targets, sits in the toolkit (section 5.7).

5.3 Baselines and targets

The arrivals targets come straight from the growth model, while the jobs and other Tier 1
targets wait for baselines that do not yet exist.

Baselines are fixed at 2025, the growth model's base year, for the growth-model indicators and at
FY2024/25 for the airport series, the recovery baseline used in Chapter 1. Targets follow two paths: the
base case, which assumes Wesgro's current activity continues with hub carriers adding capacity, and the
accelerated case, which adds direct India and China routes and dedicated activation.566 The model prints
the base case for 2028, 2030, 2032, 2034 and 2035, and the other paths for 2035 only. The 2031 base
value and the accelerated milestones are computed and marked as such.




Figure 5.2. Arrivals paths and milestones to 2035
Source: Market Assessment Report, May 2026, p19 (2025 base); Demand-Side Analysis, Jun 2026, p20, p21 (2028 and 2035 points; 1.8m
seat line). The 2031 base value is the geometric mean of the printed 2030 and 2032 points; the accelerated and conservative milestones are
computed at a constant rate between the printed 2025 and 2035 values.

Arrivals. The model's 2025 base and the airport series measure different things: arrivals on the growth
model's basis, which Skift is to confirm (section 1.1), and international passenger arrivals at Cape Town
International. The airport series is the quarterly proxy because ACSA publishes it, and it stood at 1.44m in
FY24/25 and 1.57m in FY25/26.567 Both are reported, each against its own history.

Spend. T1-03's path holds spend per arrival fixed, so its targets are in constant 2024 rand, while SA
Tourism reports nominal rand; progress is read after deflating by CPI. The model's 2024 spend of
R27.12bn sits 19% above the R22.7bn SA Tourism reported for FY2024.568 [Open item: Skift to rebase the
spend path on the FY2025 actual of R25.9bn.]

Jobs. No deliverable carries a provincial tourism employment baseline, and the Pero tourism table could
not be retrieved [provincial tourism employment baseline: Wesgro Research to supply from the Pero 2025
or the Stats SA provincial TSA]. T1-04 therefore carries no baseline or modelled path until the labour
analysis sets a provincial measure (I18).

The report restates the 300,000 as a total-sector figure for the labour analysis to test (I18). For context,
public sources put direct tourism employment at 106,707 in Cape Town in 2024569 and 953,981 nationally
in 2024.570

Green season. The 2025 month table sums to 104.9%. Normalised, it gives 61.9% for October to March
and 38.1% for the green season, in line with the printed 61% and 39% for 2024.571 T1-05 uses 38.1% as
its baseline.

Spread and community spend. No deliverable prints district visitor spend, so the share outside the
Metro has no baseline until the data protocol delivers one (I16). The community share starts from the
Inclusion Plan's traced floor; its 1% is a reference point the plan prints, not a target any party has
adopted.572

5.4 Data sources and gaps

Arrivals, seats and cruise volumes can be tracked from existing feeds; spread, bookability,
jobs and community spend cannot until new data is built or shared.

Stakeholders put data among the top barriers: no approved methodology exists for provincial tourism GDP
and jobs,573 and no one holds a single view of visitor behaviour.574 The data-sharing protocol under focus
area 18 (I16) is the route to closing the gaps below.

5.5 Tracking mechanism

A quarterly scorecard to Steerco, an annual outlook to the Board and a live dashboard
where the sources allow it.

Four principles run the tracking. Automate before collecting by hand: arrivals, seats and cruise volumes
come from feeds that already exist. Name one owner for the dashboard (the programme office) and one
for the definitions (Wesgro Research). Report every indicator against its own baseline and both paths.
Refresh the register each year at the plan review.




Figure 5.3. Quarterly scorecard layout
Source: Letsema, dashboard concept. Illustrative layout; no values shown.

Reporting runs on a fixed calendar tied to Steerco and the Board. Each month, the leads send Tier 3
outputs, initiative status and the seats and arrivals feeds to the programme office as a dashboard update.
Each quarter, the programme office takes Tier 2 outcomes where available, the risks rated high and the
escalation responses to Steerco in the scorecard. Each year, Steerco takes Tier 1 impact against both
paths, the plan refresh and gate status to the Board in an outlook report, made public at the business
outlook event. At each gate, Steerco sends the Board a gate paper on the tests in section 4.7.




Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                    155
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                        DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION




5.6 Evaluation and learning

Two evaluations bracket the plan: a mid-term review in 2030, when the base case crosses
the model's seat line, and a terminal evaluation in 2035.

The mid-term review tests whether the plan is on its path and whether the path still holds. It falls in 2030
because that is when the base case crosses the model's 1.8 million seat line,575 before runway relief on
the planning date.576 The terminal evaluation in 2035 measures the result against both paths and sets up
the next plan. Both should be run by a party independent of the programme office.

Each pillar carries two evaluation questions, one on results and one on the assumptions behind them. For
Reshape demand: did arrivals, spend per visitor and the green-season share move with the activation,
and in which markets and segments? Is conversion still the constraint, or has access overtaken it in the
growth markets? For Transform supply: did seats, readiness and bookability rise outside the Metro, and
did visitors follow? Did the airport relief arrive on time, and did the second gateways carry the load
meanwhile? For Build enabling capability: did the pilots meet their thresholds, and did community spend
rise? Did the national asks get answers, and did the data protocol deliver the baselines?

Pilots before capital. The inclusion pilots are evaluated before any capital is committed, on the Inclusion
Plan's own measures: bookings, visitors and locally retained revenue.577 A pilot scales only when it meets
the thresholds agreed before launch.578 The same rule applies to any product or asset investment under I9
and I14: test demand, then invest.

Learning into the plan. Findings from each review, gate and pilot go into the annual plan refresh under
section 4.7, so the charters, targets and sequence change with the evidence rather than at the end of the
plan.


5.7 Toolkit handover

The M&E toolkit turns this chapter into four working files that Wesgro Research owns from
handover.

The four files are the indicator register, the baseline workbook, the scorecard template and the dashboard
data model (SLA clauses 1.1.10.2 and 1.1.15.3) [clause wording to be checked against the signed
Annexure B]. The indicator register holds all 68 indicators with their definition, unit, source, frequency,
owner, baseline and target by year and path. The baseline workbook holds the baseline values with page
references to the deliverables, and the computed values with their method. Wesgro Research owns both.
The programme office owns the scorecard template, which carries the quarterly Steerco scorecard and
the annual Board outlook. It also owns the dashboard data model: the feeds, refresh frequency and
manual-entry forms for the dashboard in section 5.5.

Handover runs through knowledge-transfer sessions with Wesgro Research and the programme office, as
the SLA requires (clause 1.1.15). The Excel toolkit itself is outside this report and is listed as an open item
[M&E toolkit workbook: Letsema to deliver with the final report].

A2 Demand model

The demand model takes international arrivals from 1,498,829 in 2025 to 1,822,634,
2,175,778 or 2,516,282 in 2035, and its base case crosses the 1.8 million seat line the
model draws, at the 2030 year point.

Method
The model sets a growth rate for each source market from four levers and projects arrivals from the base
year to 2035.579 The levers are source market growth, Western Cape penetration of source-market
outbound, an airlift capacity ceiling and structural market conditions. Source-market growth sets the
ceiling: "WC cannot sustainably grow faster than it's source market is generating travellers". 580 Projections
are anchored on OAG aviation data and Skift global traveller data.581

Scenario definitions
The three scenarios vary intervention, not demand: "The difference between scenarios is not demand
potential, but the extent to which structural barriers are removed and growth opportunities are actively
pursued".582

projection.

Seat line and model limits
Both decks state that "Current air access places a hard ceiling at 1.8 million inbound seats, meaning the
projected growth cannot be realised without additional capacity or frequency".590 Neither deck prints the
seats behind the line or a load factor that converts seats into arrivals. At the 79% average load factor, 1.8
million seats would carry about 1.42 million arrivals, below the 1.57 million recorded in FY25/26, so this
report reads the line as the model's assumption, not a measured cap (section 1.3).

The model prints its seat line and six base-case year points but no seat table, annual series or group split
for the other scenarios. The seat line is 1.8 million inbound seats, drawn as a line labelled 1.8M and called

A3 Benchmark cases

Six comparators were drawn from a 30-destination screen on twelve criteria, and each case
reduces to a system type, two levers and the changes they require of the Western Cape.

Evaluation criteria
Both the Inception Report and the Global Benchmarking Report screen destinations on twelve criteria in
four groups.598 Each destination received one average score across all criteria.599

are shown as points and Valencia's second lever takes its p49 title in place of the p9 label. Melbourne's ~4.3% is a forecast average annual

System type and its three components
The report treats sustained growth as the product of three reinforcing components: institutional
coordination; strategic marketing and promotion; accessibility and connectivity.608 Without competitive
access, "latent demand does not convert".609

All six cases align coordination, marketing and access, but each builds its system around a different
anchor.610

Melbourne and Victoria: events-led. Coordination institutionalises events and a knowledge-led brand as
a cross-government economic development tool. Marketing integrates city branding, intellectual capital
and events into a single demand proposition. The event calendar is used to de-risk and stimulate aviation
demand.

Chile: connectivity-led. Coordination is anchored around a single strategic constraint, long-haul access.
Marketing runs unified national branding with defined regional identities and multi-region itinerary
packaging. Liberal aviation policy and major hub investment position Santiago as a single efficient long-
haul hub.

Kenya: product-led. Coordination aligns tourism with national priorities in conservation, trade and
diplomacy. Marketing is segment-specific and grounded in a designed product system. Access reduces
entry friction, with Nairobi as a multipurpose regional and global hub.

Singapore: orchestrated experience. A centrally orchestrated system under STB works through
structured public-private partnerships. Marketing packages experiences (events, precincts, lifestyle) into
seamless high-value propositions. Aviation, infrastructure and visitor experience are integrated into a
single seamless gateway.



608
      Global Benchmarking Report, Mar 2026, p58.
609
      Global Benchmarking Report, Mar 2026, p59.
610
      Global Benchmarking Report, Mar 2026, p61 for the system types; p59 to p60 for the three components, condensed.


Wesgro | Letsema in partnership with Skift Advisory                                                                                     169
Unlocking Tourism Growth: A Strategy for the Western Cape to 2035                                         DRAFT FOR DISCUSSION



Valencia: governance-led. Governance is decentralised and sector-led. Marketing expands the reasons
to visit: gastronomy, culture, sport, wellness and business events. Connectivity supports a multi-product
destination system and dispersal.

New Zealand: value-led. Tourism, aviation and policy are integrated around shared demand objectives.
Marketing shifts from volume to value by selling scarce, high-quality experiences. Long-haul demand
converts only when marketing, aviation and distribution are aligned.
